The Maxwell equations on full sub-extremal and extremal Kerr spacetimes
Gabriele Benomio, Rita Teixeira da Costa

Abstract
We study the Cauchy problem for the Maxwell equations in the exterior region of Kerr black hole spacetimes. The equations are formulated for components of the Maxwell field relative to the algebraically special frame of Kerr, with the unknowns treated as tensorial quantities associated with a non-integrable horizontal distribution. The extremal Maxwell components decouple into Teukolsky equations, whereas the middle Maxwell components form a coupled system of transport and elliptic equations. Assuming control over the extremal components, we prove uniform boundedness (without loss of derivatives) and decay estimates for the middle components in the full | a | ≤ M |a|\leq M range of spacetime parameters. Our analysis relies on (i) deriving a decoupled system of transport and elliptic equations for two modified middle Maxwell components and (ii) decomposing general solutions into a dynamical and stationary part, the latter determined by two real (electric and magnetic) charges which are entirely read off from the initial data at the event horizon. In the sub-extremal | a | < M |a|<M case, works of Shlapentokh-Rothman and the second author provide the necessary control over the extremal components, yielding unconditional boundedness and decay results for all the unknowns in the equations. In the extremal | a | = M |a|=M case, we formulate a conjectural boundedness and decay statement for the extremal components, motivated by work of Casals, Gralla and Zimmerman on fixed azimuthal mode solutions compactly supported away from the event horizon. Our boundedness and decay results for all the unknowns in the equations remain, therefore, conditional . We show that the complicated dynamics of the extremal components at the event horizon is inherited by the middle components; in particular, we uncover novel conservation laws for the middle components of axisymmetric solutions.



\wasyfamily

1
Introduction

The Kerr black hole family is, conjecturally, the unique family of regular stationary black hole solutions to the Einstein vacuum equations

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g)=0.

(1.1)

It is is parametrized by two real numbers,
M>0
and
|a|\leq M
. In this paper, we study the dynamics of electromagnetic waves on any fixed member of this family. Concretely, we consider the Maxwell equations

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\,\bm{F}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\star\bm{F}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(1.2)

where
\mathrm{d}
and
\star
represent the exterior derivative and Hodge dual, respectively, and where
\bm{F}
is the Faraday electromagnetic tensor, as a system of evolutionary PDEs on a fixed Kerr black hole background. Our first result concerns the sub-extremal
|a|<M
Kerr sub-family:

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix
M>0
. For any
|a|<M
, solutions of the Maxwell equations (
1.2
) on the Kerr exterior arising from suitably regular initial data

(a)

remain uniformly bounded in time in terms of initial data and,

(b)

decay in time along a suitable spacelike foliation, after subtracting a stationary solution itself determined by initial data.

The boundedness statement we obtain
does not lose derivatives
: we show that a coercive
L^{2}
-based energy flux of the Maxwell field through a suitable foliation of the Kerr exterior is controlled by an energy flux, at the same level of differentiability, of the data. In that sense, our result contains not only a suitable asymptotic stability statement in Theorem
1.1
(b), but also the orbital stability for (
1.2
) in Theorem
1.1
(a). Our results were previously known for the Schwarzschild (
a=0
) and very slowly rotating Kerr (
|a|\ll M
) sub-families by work of other authors
[
11
,
2
,
33
]
, see also
[
21
]
. In the full sub-extremal
|a|<M
range, Theorem
1.1
contains, to our knowledge, the first orbital stability for (
1.2
). Prior to this,
[
28
]
obtained a uniform sharp decay result for the Maxwell equations (
1.2
) on Kerr in the full sub-extremal
|a|<M
range, i.e. the fastest possible decay for nontrivial compactly supported initial data. The decay rates in Theorem
1.1
(b) are weaker than those of
[
28
]
as, motivated by nonlinear applications, we allow for less regular data.

As we shall describe in Section
1.1
, our strategy to prove Theorem
1.1
relies on energy boundedness and integrated local energy decay for the so-called spin
\pm 1
Teukolsky equations
[
40
]
. The spin
\pm 1
Teukolsky equations are wave-type equations for certain components of
\bm{F}
, known as
extremal
components, which are implied by (
1.2
) and decouple from the system of Maxwell equations. The analysis of the Teukolsky equations has been recently carried out in the full sub-extremal range in
[
34
,
35
]
, and we shall make use of those results here.
1
1

1

It should be noted that the earlier results of
[
28
]
for the full sub-extremal range
|a|\leq M
are
conditional
on energy boundedness and integrated local energy decay for the spin
\pm 1
Teukolsky equations. In the case
|a|\ll M
, the latter was shown by the same author in
[
27
]
.
Indeed, as in the physics tradition (see e.g.
[
14
]
), we interpret the Maxwell equations (
1.2
) as
transport
equations for the non-extremal, or
middle
, Maxwell components with forcing terms given by the extremal (Teukolsky) components. This should be contrasted with earlier works
[
11
,
2
]
for
|a|\ll M
, which are based on the Fackerell–Ipser equations
[
18
]
, and precede the introduction in
[
17
]
of the toolbox required to tackle the Teukolsky equations. We also mention an alternative approach in the
a=0
case in
[
1
]
, based on a superenergy tensor.

Before turning to a careful description of the above strategy, let us remark that our analysis of the middle Maxwell components is implemented so as to extend to the
|a|=M
extremal Kerr case. Indeed, we show:

Theorem 1.2
.

Fix
M>0
. For any
|a|\leq M
, solutions of the Maxwell equations on (
1.2
) on the Kerr exterior arising from suitably regular initial data
supported on a fixed azimuthal mode

(a)

remain uniformly bounded in time,
away from the event horizon
, in terms of initial data and,

(b)

decay in time,
away from the event horizon
, along a suitable spacelike foliation, after subtracting a stationary solution itself determined by initial data,

assuming a conjectural statement for solutions of the spin
\pm 1
Teukolsky equations (see already Conjecture
5.6
).

The added emphasis allows one to easily compare the
|a|\leq M
case with the
|a|<M
case in the previous Theorem
1.1
; evidently, Theorem
1.2
is much weaker. Indeed, even though our approach in Theorem
1.1
to deduce estimates for Maxwell (
1.2
) from estimates for Teukolsky fully generalizes to the entire Kerr black hole family
|a|\leq M
, the approach of
[
34
,
35
]
to the Teukolsky equations
does not
: in view of the growing body of work concerning waves on extremal Kerr black holes and their spherically symmetric charged analogues, we expect the behavior of solutions to the Teukolsky equation to be far worse for
|a|=M
than for
|a|<M
. The conjectured statement on which we rely in Theorem
1.2
is based on this literature, in particular on the heuristics in
[
12
,
13
,
25
]
and on the rigorous results in
[
19
]
. We direct the reader to Section
7.2
for further details and a more comprehensive comparison with the literature.

1.1
Overview of the proofs

Let us now turn to an overview of the main ideas involved in our proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. As mentioned above, following the physics tradition, we base our analysis of the Maxwell equations (
1.2
) on a black-box understanding of the spin
\pm 1
Teukolsky equations. To do so, one would like to rewrite (
1.2
) in a geometric formalism where the Teukolsky equations naturally emerge and can be directly exploited.

In
[
9
,
10
]
, the first author develops a formalism for casting geometric equations, such as (
1.1
) or (
1.2
), on
1+3
-dimensional Lorentzian manifolds in terms of local frames
\mathcal{N}=(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
where
(e_{3},e_{4})
are null vectors and the associated horizontal distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}=\langle e_{3},e_{4}\rangle^{\bot}
, for which
(e_{1},e_{2})
form a local basis, is allowed to be non-integrable. This is inspired by the mathematical literature, in particular the seminal works
[
15
,
16
]
, but also connects naturally to the physics literature. Indeed, non-integrable local frames are often used in classical physics textbooks such as
[
14
]
in the study of equations (
1.1
) or (
1.2
) on black hole spacetimes. More specifically, the
algebraically special frame

\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}
of Kerr, i.e. the choice
(e_{3},e_{4})=(e_{3}^{\rm as},e_{4}^{\rm as})
of principal null vectors, is traditionally used in the study of equations (
1.1
) (typically its linearisation) and (
1.2
) on Kerr spacetimes and is well-known to yield a non-integrable horizontal distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}=\langle e_{3}^{\rm as},e_{4}^{\rm as}\rangle^{\bot}
. The complex
s
-spin-weighted functions arising in the Newman–Penrose and Geroch–Held–Penrose formalism
[
31
,
20
]
in which these works are formulated, see e.g. the seminal papers
[
18
,
40
]
, are in
1
-to-
1
correspondence with the tensors over
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}
introduced in
[
9
,
10
]
.

The formalism of
[
9
,
10
]
showcases the remarkable hidden structure of (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) around Kerr spacetimes which are displayed by classical physics works while rewriting them in the terms of modern mathematical literature. The Maxwell equations (
1.2
) are cast into a system of first order partial differential equations for 4 unknowns—two 1-tensors,
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
, and two scalar functions,
{\bm{\rho}}
and
{\bm{\sigma}}
, on
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}
—which are frame components of the Faraday tensor
\bm{F}
relative to the frame
\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}
and make up the entirety of the degrees of freedom of
\bm{F}
. We show that the 1-tensors
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
satisfy the tensorial analogue of the spin
\pm 1
Teukolsky equations, as well as the tensorial analogue of the so-called Teukolsky–Starobinsky identities; schematically, we obtain

(
1.2
)

\displaystyle\implies(\Box_{g}+\partial)\bm{\alpha}=0,\quad(\Box_{g}-\partial)\bm{\underline{\alpha}}=0,\quad\partial^{2}_{\rm null}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\underline{\alpha}})=\partial^{2}_{\rm angular}(\bm{\underline{\alpha}},\bm{\alpha})\,.

We demonstrate that, after a very simple linear transformation
(\bm{\rho},\bm{\sigma})\mapsto(\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})
, the Maxwell equations are equivalent to the statement that all derivatives of the scalars
(\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})
are decoupled from each other (as opposed to those of the original quantities
(\bm{\rho},\bm{\sigma})
) and are fully determined by
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
and their derivatives; schematically, we have

\displaystyle\text{\eqref{eq:Maxwell-eqs-intro}}\iff\partial\widehat{\bm{\rho}}=\partial^{\leq 1}\bm{\alpha}+\partial^{\leq 1}\bm{\underline{\alpha}},\quad\partial\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}=\partial^{\leq 1}\bm{\alpha}+\partial^{\leq 1}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}.

(1.3)

These observations, with the input of
[
34
,
35
]
for
|a|<M
, and the conjectural statement for the dynamics
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
for
|a|\approx M
, already imply boundedness and decay properties for (suitably weighted) derivatives of
\bm{F}
as per Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

The system of equations (
1.2
) admits stationary (thus non-decaying) solutions
\bm{F}_{\textup{stat}}
. In general, one expects that dynamical solutions to (
1.2
) converge to a stationary solution
\bm{F}_{\textup{stat}}
, and therefore decay
to zero
of
\bm{F}
itself can only hold after subtracting a suitable
\bm{F}_{\textup{stat}}
. Stationary solutions can be fully parametrized by two topological charges
2
2

2

By standard topological arguments, for any (possibly dynamical) solution
\bm{F}
to (
1.2
), the integrals
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\star\bm{F}
and
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\bm{F}
are independent of the foliation
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-spheres on which are computed, and therefore always constant.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{E}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\star\bm{F}_{\textup{stat}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\bm{F}_{\textup{stat}}

computed over (any) foliation
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-spheres, see e.g.
[
2
, Section 3.1]
and
[
28
]
for a discussion and use of these charges. For our system of equations (
1.2
), with unknowns
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\underline{\alpha}},\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})
, stationary solutions correspond to the family of solutions such that

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha_{\textup{stat}}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\bm{\underline{\alpha}}_{\textup{stat}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\rho}}_{\textup{stat}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}_{1}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}_{\textup{stat}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}_{2}

(1.4)

on the entire Kerr exterior spacetime, where the real constants
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}
are interpreted as the two charges of the stationary solution.

It is natural to choose the stationary charges
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}
so as to coincide with the zero modes

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}\,.

(1.5)

However, contrary to the integrals of
\bm{F}
and
\star\bm{F}
over the foliation
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-spheres, the integrals (
1.5
) are, for
|a|>0
, dynamical quantities,
3
3

3

The zero modes of
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
are constant for
a=0
.
thus requiring a
choice of sphere
in (
1.5
) where the charges are computed.

Given a general solution
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\underline{\alpha}},\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})
arising from initial data prescribed on a spacelike (hyperboloidal) hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
, in order to subtract the non-zero constant to which the general
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
are converging (i.e. proving decay to zero), one may compute the charges on the
final horizon sphere
, that is

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega\,,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega\,,

where
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}=\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\Sigma_{\tau}
for spacelike hyperboloidal foliation
\Sigma_{\tau\geq 0}
. Such a choice of stationary charges immediately implies that the zero modes of
(\widehat{\bm{\rho}}-\widehat{\bm{\rho}}_{\textup{stat}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}-\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}_{\textup{stat}})
decay to zero along the event horizon, and thus
(\widehat{\bm{\rho}}-\widehat{\bm{\rho}}_{\textup{stat}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}-\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}_{\textup{stat}})
themselves decay to zero along the event horizon in the full
|a|\leq M
range. The a-priori control over
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
along the event horizon provided by our approach may be compared to possibly different approaches to the analysis of the Maxwell equations on black hole spacetimes in which control over the middle Maxwell components along the event horizon first requires control of these quantities along hypersurfaces located far from the event horizon (see e.g.
[
28
]
in the context of establishing sharp decay rates for the Maxwell equations on Kerr spacetimes).

Let us pause to highlight an important byproduct of our choice to identify
(\widehat{\bm{\rho}}_{\rm stat},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}_{\rm stat})
at the event horizon.
In linear problems, one expects to be able to choose stationary charges entirely at the level of initial data (as opposed to nonlinear problems, where modulation procedures are typically necessary). To that end, we prove that our charges
(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2})
, originally identified teleologically, can actually be read off from the initial data for the Maxwell system:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Q}_{1}(0)\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Q}_{2}(0)\,,

where we have introduced scalar functions
\mathfrak{Q}_{1,2}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined through

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{I_{a,M}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\left(\frac{r_{+}^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}\,\widehat{\bm{\rho}}+\frac{2ar_{+}\cos\theta}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}\,\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}-\frac{1}{2}(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\left(\mathfrak{k},\widetilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right)\right)d\Omega\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{I_{a,M}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\left(\frac{r_{+}^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}\,\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}-\frac{2ar_{+}\cos\theta}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}\,\widehat{\bm{\rho}}+\frac{1}{2}(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\mathfrak{k}\wedge\widetilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right)d\Omega\,.

Here
I_{a,M}\neq 0
is a constant and
\mathfrak{k}
is an explicit
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}
one-form depending only
a,M
, and such that
\mathfrak{k}=0
for
a=0
;
\widetilde{\bm{\alpha}}
is a
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}
tensor obtained as the projection of
\bm{\alpha}
over the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}
-spheres via the
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-projection procedure of
[
10
]
. Note that, for
a=0
,
\mathfrak{Q}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{2}
reduce to the zero modes of
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
. The a-posteriori retracing of our charges to the initial data relies on two horizon conservation laws

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{Q}_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{Q}_{2}

\displaystyle=0\,.

That these hold crucially depends on the enhanced properties of the algebraically special frame and

\mathbb{S}^{2}
-projection procedure at the event horizon (see Sections
2.2
and
2.4
below): no such conservation laws hold away from the event horizon.

In the sub-extremal
|a|<M
case, the horizon decay of
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
can be extended to decay off the horizon by a standard integration of the transport equations (
1.3
) in the direction transversal to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. This extension exploits the decay of
both

\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
(and their derivatives) along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. On the other hand, in the extremal
|a|=M
case, one does
not
expect decay of both
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. In particular,
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
is expected to grow in time along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(see e.g.
[
12
,
25
,
19
]
), thus preventing one from extending decay of
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
off
\mathcal{H}^{+}
via the standard integration procedure adopted for the
|a|<M
case.

To circumvent this obstruction, we develop a strategy to integrate the transport equations (
1.3
) which only requires
degenerate
-at-the-horizon control of
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
. The crucial observation is that the transport equations (
1.3
) can be combined with the Teukolsky equations to obtain, schematically,

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})=\partial_{r}\underline{\bm{\Phi}}+\text{faster decaying terms},

(1.6)

at the future event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(see already Section
6.4.1
for the precise computation in a simpler setting) and, for
|a|=M
,
\underline{\bm{\Phi}}
denotes the derivative of
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
along the null generator of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Note that, in view of the work in
[
7
,
26
,
5
]
on conservation laws for axisymmetric solutions of the Teukolsky equations along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, this pointwise identity immediately implies

Proposition 1.3
.

For axisymmetric solutions of the Maxwell system on extremal Kerr (
a=M
), there are conservation laws for transversal derivatives of
(\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

See Remark
6.5
for a precise statement. On the other hand, for general solutions, we recall from the heuristics
[
12
,
25
]
that while one expects
\partial_{r}\underline{\bm{\Phi}}
to
grow
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, one expects
\underline{\bm{\Phi}}
to decay. Integrating the pointwise identity (
1.6
), we deduce

\displaystyle(\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})=(\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}+\underline{\bm{\Phi}}+\text{faster decaying terms}.

Thus, decay of
(\widehat{\bm{\rho}},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}})
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is propagated to the entire Kerr exterior manifold.

1.2
Related works and outlook

We conclude this introduction by making contact with related problems in the literature. We have briefly mentioned applications of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
to nonlinear problems. The simplest such problems are nonlinear theories of electromagnetism, such as the Maxwell–Born–Infeld theory, on Kerr spacetimes. This was previously considered by
[
32
]
in the
a=0
case.

A more challenging source of nonlinear problems comes from coupling electromagnetism to gravity through the Einstein–Maxwell system

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\mathrm{Ric}(\bm{g})=2\,\bm{F}\cdot\bm{F}^{\bm{\sharp}_{2}}-\frac{1}{2}|\bm{F}|^{2}_{\bm{g}}\,\bm{g}\,,\\
\mathrm{d}\,\bm{F}=0,\quad\mathrm{d}\star\bm{F}=0\,.\end{gathered}

Within the Einstein–Maxwell system, Kerr black holes sit as the
Q=0
sub-family of the wider Kerr–Newman family of black hole solutions, which is parametrized by
(a,M,Q)
with
Q>0
and
a^{2}+Q^{2}\leq M^{2}
. While it might be tempting to extrapolate from Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
consequences for the Einstein–Maxwell system (
1.2
), we caution the reader that the Maxwell equations (
1.2
) are
not
the linearisation of (
1.2
) around Kerr(–Newmann). Indeed, by linearising around the Kerr–Newman family, one obtains an intricate system of coupled gravitational and electromagnetic perturbations. In the
a=0
case, this linearised system has been extensively studied in
[
23
]
(
|Q|<M
) and
[
5
]
(
|Q|=M
, dynamics of the extremal components). For the nonlinear dynamics in the
|Q|=M
case under spherical symmetry—where, in view of the rigidity of (
1.2
), one needs to add a scalar field to the system as an additional degree of freedom— a rather complete picture has been obtained in
[
4
]
; see also the earlier numerics
[
30
]
. For
a\neq 0
, though much less is known, we direct the reader to the works
[
24
,
22
]
for substantial recent progress. In this direction, we also mention the works
[
38
,
29
]
which consider the Maxwell equations on more general spacetimes motivated by perturbations of black holes.

1.3
Outline of the paper

We briefly outline the structure of the paper:

•

Sections
2
.
We introduce the Kerr exterior manifold, the algebraically special frame and the differentiable structures which will be useful for the sequel.

•

Sections
3
.
We derive the Maxwell equations on a general spacetime. The equations are formulated for components of the Maxwell field relative to a general (possibly non-integrable) null frame. We then specialize the equations to the Kerr exterior manifold and discuss the notion of seed initial data and prove well-posedness of the Cauchy problem.

•

Section
4
.
We introduce the energy flux norms which will be used in the analysis.

•

Section
5
.
We state the main theorems of the paper.

•

Section
6
.
This is the core of the paper. We prove boundedness and decay estimates on the middle Maxwell components in terms of suitable energy flux norms for the extremal Maxwell components. We also derive a precise version of the identity (
1.6
) valid in the full
|a|\leq M
range. As a direct application, we obtain conservation laws along the event horizon for axisymmetric solutions to the Maxwell equations in the extremal
|a|=M
case.

•

Section
7
.
We state the boundedness and decay estimates for the extremal Maxwell components. In the
|a|<M
case, these are taken from
[
34
,
35
]
as a black box. In the
|a|=M
case, these were previously formulated as a conjecture in Section
5
that we now motivate.

•

Section
8
.
We combine the results of Sections
6
–
7
to obtain the proof of our main theorems stated in Section
5
.

•

Appendix
A
.
We derive several identities from the Maxwell equations, such as the tensorial Teukolsky–Starobinsky identities, the tensorial Teukolsky equations and the tensorial Fackerell–Ipser equations. We also rewrite the Maxwell equations for transformed spin-weighted quantities.

2
The Kerr exterior manifold

In this section, we introduce the Kerr exterior manifold, its algebraically special frame and the spacetime foliations which will be necessary in our analysis.

2.1
Definition

We define the manifold-with-boundary

\mathcal{M}:=(-\infty,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}

(2.1)

with coordinates
\bar{t}\in(-\infty,\infty)
,
y\in[0,\infty)
and standard (local) spherical coordinates
(\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
. We define the
(future) event horizon
as the boundary of
\mathcal{M}
,

\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\partial\mathcal{M}=\{y=0\}\,,

and
(future) null infinity
as the formal hypersurface

\mathcal{I}^{+}:=\left\{\bar{t}=\infty\right\}\,.

We define the vector fields

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\bar{t}}\,,

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}

(2.2)

on
\mathcal{M}
. We will denote by
\widetilde{Z}
the smooth (degenerate) extension of
Z
to a global vector field on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

Given real parameters
a
and
M
, with
|a|\leq M
, we define the positive constants

r_{\pm}:=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}

and a new coordinate
\bar{r}=\bar{r}(y)
such that

\bar{r}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[r_{+},\infty)\,,

with
\bar{r}(3M)=y(3M)
. The refer to the coordinates

(\bar{t},\bar{r},\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi})

as
Kerr coordinates
. We note that
\mathcal{H}^{+}=\left\{\bar{r}=r_{+}\right\}
in Kerr coordinates.

For fixed
|a|\leq M
, we define the smooth scalar functions

\displaystyle\Delta(\bar{r})

\displaystyle:=(\bar{r}-r_{+})(\bar{r}-r_{-})\,,

\displaystyle\Sigma(\bar{r},\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle:=\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}

on
\mathcal{M}
and define the
Kerr family of metrics
as the two-parameter family of Lorentzian metrics
g_{a,M}
on
\mathcal{M}
such that

\displaystyle g_{a,M}=

\displaystyle-\left(1-\frac{2M\bar{r}}{\Sigma}\right){d\bar{t}}^{2}+2\,d\bar{t}\,d\bar{r}+\Sigma\,{d\bar{\theta}}{}^{2}+\frac{(\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta\,a^{2}\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,{d\bar{\phi}}{}^{2}

(2.3)

\displaystyle-2\,a\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,d\bar{r}\,d\bar{\phi}-\frac{4aM\bar{r}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,d\bar{t}\,d\bar{\phi}\,.

The metric
g_{a,M}
in (
2.3
) is manifestly
smooth
on
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
can be checked to be a
null
hypersurface relative to
g_{a,M}
, whereas the level sets of
r_{+}<\bar{r}<\infty
are
timelike
hypersurfaces. The smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
will be referred to as the
Kerr exterior manifold
. One can check that
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
solves the vacuum Einstein equations, i.e.
g_{a,M}
is Ricci-flat. In the sequel, the Kerr metric will be denoted by
g
.

2.1.1
The Kerr-star and Boyer–Lindquist differentiable structures

For fixed
|a|\leq M
, we consider a smooth scalar function
\iota=\iota(\bar{r})
such that

\iota:[r_{+},\infty)\rightarrow[0,1]

and

\iota(\bar{r})=\begin{cases}1,\quad r_{+}\leq\bar{r}\leq 17M/8\\
0,\quad\bar{r}\geq 9M/4\end{cases}\,,

(2.4)

with

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-\iota(\bar{r})\left(\frac{\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-1\right)>0\,,

\displaystyle 2-\left(1-\frac{2M\bar{r}}{\Sigma}\right)\left(\frac{\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-\iota(\bar{r})\left(\frac{\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-1\right)\right)>0\,.

We define
Kerr-star coordinates
4
4

4

With a slight abuse of notation, we do not rename the coordinates
(\bar{r},\bar{\theta})
.

(t^{*},\bar{r},\bar{\theta},\phi^{*})

(2.5)

on
\mathcal{M}
such that

\displaystyle t^{*}

\displaystyle=\bar{t}-\int_{r_{0}}^{\bar{r}}\left[(1-\iota(\bar{r}{}^{\prime}))\,\frac{{\bar{r}{}^{\prime}}{}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta(\bar{r}{}^{\prime})}+\iota(\bar{r}{}^{\prime})\right]d\bar{r}{}^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle\phi^{*}

\displaystyle=\bar{\phi}-\int_{r_{0}}^{\bar{r}}(1-\iota(\bar{r}{}^{\prime}))\,\frac{a}{\Delta(\bar{r}{}^{\prime})}\,d\bar{r}{}^{\prime}\quad\text{mod}\,2\pi\,,

with
r_{0}=9M/4
. It follows that, for the vector fields introduced in (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\partial_{{t}^{*}}\,,

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\partial_{{\phi}^{*}}\,.

The Kerr-star coordinates (
2.5
) are
global
(modulo the usual degeneration of the angular coordinates
(\bar{\theta},\phi^{*})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
) coordinates on
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. We define the foliation Kerr-star two-spheres

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}:=\left\{t^{*},\bar{r}\right\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

For any finite
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, the hypersurface

\mathcal{S}_{\tau}:=\left\{t^{*}=\tau\right\}

(2.6)

is a
spacelike
,
5
5

5

This property can be easily checked and it holds in view of the definition of the function
\iota(\bar{r})
.
asymptotically flat hypersurface intersecting
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Future null infinity corresponds to the formal hypersurface
\left\{t^{*}=\infty\right\}=\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

The Kerr-star coordinates (
2.5
) can be related to
Boyer–Lindquist coordinates

(t,r,\theta,\phi)

on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{H}^{+}
by the coordinate transformation

\displaystyle t^{*}

\displaystyle=t+\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\iota(r^{\prime})\,\left(\frac{{r^{\prime}}{}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}-1\right)\,dr^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{r}

\displaystyle=r\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}

\displaystyle=\theta\,,

\displaystyle\phi^{*}

\displaystyle=\phi+\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\iota(r^{\prime})\,\frac{a}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}\,dr^{\prime}\quad\textup{mod}\,2\pi\,.

In Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, the Kerr metric takes the familiar form

g=-\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\,(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta\,d\phi)^{2}+\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\,dr^{2}+\Sigma\,d\theta^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\,(a\,dt-(r^{2}+a^{2})\,d\phi)^{2}\,.

(2.7)

2.1.2
A hyperboloidal differentiable structure

Let
\tilde{\zeta}\colon[r_{+},\infty)\to[0,1]
is a smooth cut-off function which vanishes for
r\leq 3M
and is equal to 1 for
r\geq 4M
. We define
hyperboloidal Kerr-star coordinates
6
6

6

With a slight abuse of notation, we do not rename the coordinates
(r,\theta)
.

(\tilde{t}{}^{*},r,\theta,\tilde{\phi}{}^{*})

on
\mathcal{M}
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{t}{}^{*}

\displaystyle=t^{*}-f(r)\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}{}^{*}

\displaystyle=\phi^{*}

for

\displaystyle f(r):=\tilde{\zeta}(r)\left(r+2M\log r-\frac{3M^{2}}{r}\right).

In hyperboloidal Kerr-star coordinates, we have

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tilde{t}{}^{*}},

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tilde{\phi}{}^{*}}.

For future convenience, we define the vector field

\tilde{X}:=\partial_{r}|_{(\tilde{t}{}^{*},r,\theta,\tilde{\phi}{}^{*})}\,,

(2.8)

for which one can check that
\tilde{X}=\partial_{r}|_{(t,r,\theta,\phi)}+\frac{df}{dr}T
for
r\geq 9M/4
. We also define the foliation hyperboloidal two-spheres

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tilde{t}{}^{*},r}:=\{\tilde{t}{}^{*},r\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

For any finite
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, the hypersurface

\Sigma_{\tau}:=\{\tilde{t}{}^{*}=\tau\}

(2.9)

is a spacelike hypersurface intersecting
\mathcal{H}^{+}
which becomes
asymptotically null
as it approaches
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

Figure 1
:
\Sigma_{\tau}
are asymptotically null, smooth and foliate
\mathcal{M}
.

2.2
The algebraically special horizontal distribution

2.2.1
The algebraically special frame

In Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, we define the
algebraically special
null vector fields

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{t}+\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{r}+\frac{a}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{\phi}\,,

(2.10)

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\text{as}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}\,\partial_{t}-\partial_{r}+\frac{a}{\Delta}\,\partial_{\phi}\,.

(2.11)

The vector fields
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
and
e_{3}^{\text{as}}
are regular and non-degenerate vector fields on the entire manifold
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. We also define the induced (global, regular) horizontal distribution

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}:=\left\langle e^{\text{as}}_{3},e^{\text{as}}_{4}\right\rangle^{\perp}

on
\mathcal{M}
. One can complete the null vector fields (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) with a local orthonormal basis

(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})

of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
to form the local null frame

\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}=(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2},e^{\text{as}}_{3},e^{\text{as}}_{4})\,,

called the
algebraically special frame
of the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
.

The frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
is
non-integrable
for
|a|>0
, with
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
a
non-integrable
distribution, see for instance
[
10
, Definition 4.7]
. Along the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
has some noteworthy properties:

•

We have

e_{4}^{\text{as}}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}=2\,\frac{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma(r_{+})}\,T+2\,\frac{a}{\Sigma(r_{+})}\,\widetilde{Z}\,,

(2.12)

i.e. the vector field
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
is tangent to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and spanned by the Killing vector fields
T
and
\widetilde{Z}
.

•

We have

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\subset T\mathcal{H}^{+}\,,

i.e. the arbitrary local frame
(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
is tangent to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Thus, the vector fields

(e^{\text{as}}_{4},e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})

form a local basis of the
integrable
distribution
T\mathcal{H}^{+}\subset T\mathcal{M}
.

For future convenience, we note a possible explicit choice of
(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})
which reads

\displaystyle e_{1}^{\text{as}}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}\sin\theta\cos\theta}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{t}+\frac{r}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{\theta}+\frac{a\cos\theta}{\Sigma\sin\theta}\,\partial_{\phi}\,,

\displaystyle e_{2}^{\text{as}}

\displaystyle=\frac{ar\sin\theta}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{t}-\frac{a\cos\theta}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{\theta}+\frac{r}{\Sigma\sin\theta}\,\partial_{\phi}

(2.13)

and we define

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\frac{\Sigma}{r^{2}+a^{2}}e_{4}^{\rm as}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}e_{3}^{\rm as}\,.

(2.14)

2.2.2
Induced metric and connection coefficients

The induced metric
\not{g}
(together with its inverse
\not{g}^{-1}
and associated standard volume form
\not{\varepsilon}
) over
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
and the connection coefficients of
g
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
are defined as
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors
(see
[
10
, Definition 4.9]
). The
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors are defined globally on
\mathcal{M}
and are regular quantities on the entire manifold
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

In Boyer--Lindquist coordinates, the induced metric reads
7
7

7

Strictly speaking, the quantities (
2.15
)–(
2.18
) below are canonical extensions of the induced metric and connection coefficients (see
[
10
, Definition 4.17]
).

\not{g}=\frac{a^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,dt^{2}-2\,\frac{a\,(r^{2}+a^{2})}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,dt\,d\phi+\Sigma\,d\theta^{2}+\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,d\phi^{2}

(2.15)

and the connection coefficients
\eta
,
\underline{\eta}
and
\zeta
of
g
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
read

\displaystyle\eta=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{a^{2}r}{\Sigma^{2}}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,dt-\frac{a^{2}\sin(2\theta)}{2\,\Sigma}\,d\theta+\frac{ar(r^{2}+a^{2})}{\Sigma^{2}}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,d\phi\,,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\underline{\eta}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{a^{2}r}{\Sigma^{2}}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,dt-\frac{a^{2}\sin(2\theta)}{2\,\Sigma}\,d\theta-\frac{ar(r^{2}+a^{2})}{\Sigma^{2}}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,d\phi\,,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\zeta=

\displaystyle\,\eta\,.

(2.18)

The remaining connection coefficients of
g
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
read

\displaystyle{\widehat{\chi}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)

\displaystyle=\frac{2a\Delta\cos\theta}{\Sigma^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{2a\cos\theta}{\Sigma}\,,

\displaystyle(\text{tr}\chi)

\displaystyle=\frac{2r\Delta}{\Sigma^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2r}{\Sigma}

and

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\left(a^{2}(M-r)\cos^{2}\theta+a^{2}r-Mr^{2}\right)}{\Sigma^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\underline{\omega}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\xi}

\displaystyle=0\,.

The Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
of
g
induces a connection
\not{\nabla}
over
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
(see
[
10
, Section 4.4.1]
for its rigorous definition). The induced connection
\not{\nabla}_{a,M}
is compatible with the induced metric
\not{g}
, however it fails to be torsion-free (and thus Levi-Civita relative to
\not{g}
) because the distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
is non-integrable.

2.2.3
Tensor products and horizontal differential operators

In this section, we recall the definition of contractions and products of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors and the differential operators acting on
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors. In our notation, the Latin capital letters are horizontal frame indices.

Given a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-form
\varsigma
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
two-tensor
\theta
, we introduce the notation
\varsigma^{\sharp}
,
\theta^{\sharp_{1}}
and
\theta^{\sharp_{2}}
such that

\displaystyle{\varsigma}^{\sharp}{}^{A}

\displaystyle=\not{g}^{AB}\varsigma_{B}\,,

\displaystyle{\theta}^{\sharp_{1}}{}_{A}^{B}

\displaystyle=\not{g}^{BC}\theta_{CA}\,,

\displaystyle{\theta}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{B}

\displaystyle=\not{g}^{BC}\theta_{AC}\,.

We define the duality relations

\displaystyle{{}^{\star}\varsigma}_{A}

\displaystyle={\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}}{}^{B}_{A}\,\varsigma_{B}\,,

\displaystyle{{}^{\star}\theta}_{AB}

\displaystyle={\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}}{}^{C}_{A}\,\theta_{BC}\,.

By definition, we have
{{{}^{\star}}{{}^{\star}}\varsigma}=-\varsigma
.

Given the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-forms
\varsigma
and
\tilde{\varsigma}
, we define the products

\displaystyle\varsigma\cdot\tilde{\varsigma}=(\varsigma,\tilde{\varsigma})

\displaystyle=\not{g}^{AB}\varsigma_{A}\,\tilde{\varsigma}_{B}\,,

\displaystyle\varsigma\wedge\tilde{\varsigma}

\displaystyle=\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}\varsigma_{A}\,\tilde{\varsigma}_{B}\,.

The connection
\not{\nabla}
can be employed to introduce a set of differential operators acting on
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors, as follows. For any smooth scalar function
f
,
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-form
\varsigma
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
two-tensor
\theta
, we define the divergence operator

\displaystyle\not{\text{div}}\,\varsigma

\displaystyle=\not{g}^{AB}(\not{\nabla}_{A}\varsigma)_{B}\,,

\displaystyle(\not{\text{div}}\,\theta)_{A}

\displaystyle=\not{g}^{CB}(\not{\nabla}_{C}\theta)_{AB}\,,

the curl operator

\not{\text{curl}}\,\varsigma=\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}(\not{\nabla}_{A}\varsigma)_{B}

and the horizontal scalar and covariant Laplacian

\displaystyle\not{\Delta}f

\displaystyle=\not{\text{div}}\not{\nabla}f\,,

\displaystyle\not{\Delta}\varsigma

\displaystyle=\not{\text{div}}\not{\nabla}\varsigma\,.

The identities
\not{\Delta}f=-\not{\text{curl}}{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}f
and
\not{\Delta}\varsigma=-\not{\text{curl}}{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}\varsigma
hold.

2.2.4
Notable geometric identities

In this section, we collect some geometric identities which will be useful for the sequel.

On
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, we have

\not{\nabla}_{4}\Gamma|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}=0

for any connection coefficient
\Gamma
of
g
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
. In particular,

\hat{\omega}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}=0\quad\quad\text{when $|a|=M$.}

(2.19)

The identity (
2.19
) encodes the fact that the surface gravity of the extremal Kerr event horizon is zero.

On
\mathcal{M}
, by using the explicit formulas of Section
2.2.2
, we can compute

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}\Sigma=\Sigma(\eta+\underline{\eta})\,,\qquad\not{\nabla}(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)=-\not{\nabla}\Sigma\,,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\left(\Sigma^{-1}\not{\nabla}\Sigma\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi(\eta+\underline{\eta})+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}(\eta+\underline{\eta})\,,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}\tr\underline{\chi}=-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\,,\qquad\not{\nabla}\tr\chi=\not{\nabla}\left(-\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\tr\underline{\chi}\right)=(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})-\tr\chi(\eta+\underline{\eta})\,.

The Codazzi equations
[
10
, Eqs. (96)–(97)]
yield

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})

\displaystyle={}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(-\tr\underline{\chi})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(2\underline{\eta}-\eta)+\tr\underline{\chi}{}^{\star}\eta

\displaystyle=\tr\underline{\chi}{}^{\star}\eta-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\underline{\eta}\,,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})

\displaystyle={}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(-\tr\chi)-3(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\eta-\tr\chi{}^{\star}\eta

\displaystyle=\tr\chi{}^{\star}\underline{\eta}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})(2\eta+\underline{\eta})\,.

(2.22)

The transport equations
[
10
, Eqs. (90), (91) and (93)]
for
\eta
and
\underline{\eta}
give

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}(\eta-\underline{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})(-3\eta+\underline{\eta})-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})({}^{\star}\eta+{}^{\star}\underline{\eta})\,,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\eta

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi(\eta-\underline{\eta})+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\,,

from which we obtain by direct computation

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\eta}=-\not{\nabla}_{4}\eta+\not{\nabla}_{4}\left(\Sigma^{-1}\not{\nabla}\Sigma\right)=-\tr\chi\underline{\eta}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}\underline{\eta}=-2\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\underline{\eta}\,.

The transport equations
[
10
, Eqs. (84), (85), (88) and (89)]
for
\tr\underline{\chi}
,
\tr\chi
,
(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})
and
(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})
yield

\displaystyle 2\curlsl\eta

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}_{3}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})+2\sigma_{G}\,,

\displaystyle 2\divsl\eta

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}_{3}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\tr\underline{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})-2|\eta|^{2}-2\rho_{G}\,,

where

\displaystyle\rho_{G}

\displaystyle=\frac{2Mr}{\Sigma^{3}}(3a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta-r^{2}),

\displaystyle\sigma_{G}

\displaystyle=\frac{2Ma\cos\theta}{\Sigma^{3}}(3r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)

are components of the Weyl tensor of
g

[
10
, Section 5.4]
, and

\displaystyle\curlsl(\eta+\underline{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\not{\nabla}_{3}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})+\not{\nabla}_{4}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})+\hat{\omega}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\right]

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\divsl(\eta-\underline{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\not{\nabla}_{3}\tr\chi-\not{\nabla}_{4}\tr\underline{\chi}-\hat{\omega}\tr\underline{\chi}\right]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2.23)

where the final equality is obtained by direct computation. Finally, we note the identity

(\eta,\eta)-(\underline{\eta},\underline{\eta})=0\,,

which can be checked by direct computation.

For the vector field (
2.8
), an easy computation yields

\displaystyle\tilde{X}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}e_{4}^{\rm as}-\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\right)e_{3}^{\rm as}-\frac{2r}{\Sigma}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot\not{\nabla}

(2.24)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{df}{dr}-1\right)\left[\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}e_{3}^{\rm as}+\frac{1}{2}e_{4}^{\rm as}-\frac{2r}{\Sigma}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot\not{\nabla}\right]

for
r\geq 9M/4
.

2.3
Induced structure and norms over foliation spheres

In this section, we introduce angular operators and norms over the foliation spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}
and
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tilde{t}^{*},r}
. We take
\bigcirc
to be a placeholder for
(t^{*},\bar{r})
or
(\tilde{t}^{*},r)
, particularizing to each case only when relevant.

We denote by
(\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}},\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}})
the metric and associated Levi-Civita connection induced by the Kerr metric and Levi-Civita connection
(g,\nabla)
over the foliation spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}
. We associate the standard volume forms

\displaystyle\not{\varepsilon}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\det\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}}}d\bar{\theta}\,d\phi^{*},

\displaystyle\not{\varepsilon}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tilde{t}^{*},r}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\det\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tilde{t}^{*},r}}}d\theta\,d\tilde{\phi}^{*}.

For later convenience, we also define the rescaled volume forms

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{4\pi}{\text{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc})}\,\not{\varepsilon}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}\,.

For any
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}

k
-tensor
\varsigma
,
8
8

8

The notion of a
\mathbb{S}^{2}

k
-tensor is here understood in the sense of
[
16
]
(i.e. as a smooth section of
\otimes_{k}(T\mathbb{S}^{2})^{\star}
).
we define the pointwise norm

\displaystyle|\varsigma|_{{\not{g}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}}^{2}

\displaystyle:={\not{g}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}^{A_{1}B_{1}}\cdots{\not{g}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}^{A_{k}B_{k}}\varsigma_{A_{1}\cdots A_{k}}\varsigma_{B_{1}\cdots B_{k}}\,,

where the contracting indices are frame indices relative to an orthonormal (relative to
{\not{g}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}
) frame of
T\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}
, and the
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc})
-norm

\left\lVert\varsigma\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc})}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}|\varsigma|_{{\not{g}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}}^{2}\mathring{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}\,.

(2.25)

For any
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}

k
-tensor
\varpi
, we define the pointwise norm

\displaystyle|\varpi|_{{\not{g}}}^{2}

\displaystyle:={\not{g}}^{A_{1}B_{1}}\cdots{\not{g}}^{A_{k}B_{k}}\varpi_{A_{1}\cdots A_{k}}\varpi_{B_{1}\cdots B_{k}}\,,

where the contracting indices are frame indices relative to an orthonormal (relative to
\not{g}
) frame of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}
, and the
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc})
-norm

\left\lVert\varpi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc})}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}|\varpi|_{{\not{g}}}^{2}\,\mathring{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}\,.

(2.26)

We note that the norms (
2.25
)–(
2.26
) do not introduce any additional (with respect to the pointwise norm inside the integral) weight in
\bar{r},r
as
\bar{r},r\rightarrow\infty
. Indeed, if we let
d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}
denote the volume form induced by the round metric over the spheres
\mathbb{S}_{\bigcirc}^{2}
once the round metric is rescaled by the inverse square of the (area-)radius of the sphere (informally, consider
d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}
to be the volume form induced by the round metric on the
unit
sphere), then there exist universal constants
C,c>0
such that

\displaystyle c\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}|\varsigma|_{\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}}^{2}d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}\leq\left\lVert\varsigma\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc})}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}|\varsigma|_{\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}}^{2}d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}\,,

(2.27)

\displaystyle c\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}|\varpi|_{\not{g}}^{2}\,d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}\leq\left\lVert\varpi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc})}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}|\varpi|_{\not{g}}^{2}\,d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bigcirc}}\,.

(2.28)

In particular, we note that

\displaystyle d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}}

\displaystyle=\sin\bar{\theta}\,d\bar{\theta}d\phi^{*},

\displaystyle d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tilde{t}{}^{*},r}}

\displaystyle=\sin\theta\,d\theta d\tilde{\phi}^{*}.

For later convenience, one can check that, at the event horizon, the identity

\not{\varepsilon}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r_{+}}}=(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})\,d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r_{+}}}

(2.29)

holds. In what follows, we will often drop the subscripts from the pointwise norms and volume forms whenever doing so does not cause ambiguity.

2.4
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-projection on the event horizon

In the previous section, we introduced the metric and associated Levi-Civita connection
(\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}},\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}})
induced by
(g,\nabla)
over the foliation spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}
. We now let
\check{\not{g}}
be the metric induced by
\not{g}
over the foliation spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}
via the projection procedure in
[
10
, Section 7]
, and denote by
\not{\varepsilon}_{\check{\not{g}}}
its associated standard volume form. We remark that

\check{\not{g}}\neq\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}}

on
\mathcal{M}
. We denote the Levi-Civita connection of
\check{\not{g}}
by
\check{\not{\nabla}}
. We quote from
[
10
, Section 7]
the properties of
(\check{\not{g}},\check{\not{\nabla}})
which will be exploited in our analysis:

Proposition 2.1

(
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-projection formulae)
.

For any
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}
one-form
\varsigma
, let
\tilde{\varsigma}
be the associated
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}
one-form such that

\displaystyle\tilde{\varsigma}(e_{A}^{\textup{ad}})

\displaystyle=g(e_{A}^{\textup{ad}},e_{B}^{\textup{as}})\varsigma^{B}\,,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\left\{1,2\right\}\,,

with
(e_{1}^{\textup{ad}},e_{2}^{\textup{ad}})
an arbitrary local frame of
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}
. Then,
\tilde{\varsigma}
satisfies the property

|\tilde{\varsigma}|_{\check{\not{g}}}=|\varsigma|_{\not{g}}\,.

(2.30)

Furthermore,
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, we have

\check{\not{g}}=\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r_{+}}}

(2.31)

and the projection formulae

\displaystyle\widetilde{\not{\nabla}_{4}f}

\displaystyle=\check{\not{\nabla}}_{4}f\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\not{\nabla}_{3}f}

\displaystyle=\check{\not{\nabla}}_{3}f\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\not{\nabla}f}

\displaystyle=\check{\not{\nabla}}f+\frac{1}{2}(\check{\not{\nabla}}_{4}f)\mathfrak{k}

for any smooth scalar function
f
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\not{\nabla}\varsigma}

\displaystyle=\check{\not{\nabla}}\,\widetilde{\varsigma}+\frac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{k}\otimes\check{\not{\nabla}}_{4}\,\widetilde{\varsigma}\implies\begin{cases}\widetilde{\not{\textup{div}}\,\varsigma}&=\check{\not{\textup{div}}}\,\widetilde{\varsigma}+\frac{1}{2}\,\left(\mathfrak{k},\check{\not{\nabla}}_{4}\,\widetilde{\varsigma}\right)_{\check{\not{g}}}\,,\\
\widetilde{\not{\textup{curl}}\,\varsigma}&=\check{\not{\textup{curl}}}\,\widetilde{\varsigma}+\frac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{k}\wedge_{\check{\not{g}}}\check{\not{\nabla}}_{4}\,\widetilde{\varsigma}\,,\end{cases}

for any
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}
one-form
\varsigma
. Here,
\mathfrak{k}
is a
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}
one-form such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}(e_{A}^{\rm ad})

\displaystyle=g(e_{3}^{\rm as},e_{A}^{\rm ad})\,,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\left\{1,2\right\}\,,

with
(e_{1}^{\textup{ad}},e_{2}^{\textup{ad}})
an arbitrary local frame of
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}
. It can be checked that
\not{\nabla}_{4}\mathfrak{k}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}=0
. One has
\mathfrak{k}=0
when
|a|=0
.

We remark that, in view of the identity (
2.31
), one can replace the connection
\check{\not{\nabla}}
with
\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}}
(and similarly for the associated angular operators) and the tensor products relative to
\check{\not{g}}
with tensor products relative to
\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},\bar{r}}}
in the horizon projection formulae stated in Proposition
2.1
. Combining the identities (
2.29
) and (
2.31
), it also follows that

\not{\varepsilon}_{\check{\not{g}}}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}=(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})\,d\Omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r_{+}}}\,.

(2.32)

More complicated projection formulae hold off of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, see e.g.
[
10
,
8
]
. On
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the properties of
\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}
noted in Section
2.2.1
lead to the simpler formulae stated in Proposition
2.1
.

3
The Maxwell equations

In this section, we first derive the Maxwell equations on a general spacetime. We then specialise the system of equations to the Kerr exterior manifold and discuss special classes of solutions, the notion of initial data and its well-posedness.

3.1
The equations on a general spacetime

In this section, we derive the (source-free) Maxwell equations for the Maxwell field components relative to a general null frame of a general Lorentzian manifold. In particular, the frame is not assumed to be integrable. To derive the equations, we apply the formalism for non-integrable horizontal distributions from
[
10
, Section 4]
. Here, we adopt the boldface notation to denote both the unknowns and the background quantities in the system of equations.

Let
(\bm{\mathcal{M}},\bm{g})
be a
(3+1)
-dimensional, smooth, time-oriented Lorentzian manifold endowed with a local null frame
\bm{\mathcal{N}}=(\bm{e}_{\bm{1}},\bm{e}_{\bm{2}},\bm{e}_{\bm{3}},\bm{e}_{\bm{4}})
, with associated horizontal distribution
\bm{\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}}
. We denote by
\bm{\nabla}
the Levi-Civita connection of
\bm{g}
on
\bm{\mathcal{M}}
. Let
\bm{F}\in\Lambda^{2}(\bm{\mathcal{M}})
. Consider the
\bm{\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}}
one-forms
\bm{\alpha},\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
and the smooth scalar functions
\bm{\rho},\bm{\sigma}
on
\bm{\mathcal{M}}
defined by

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}(\bm{e_{A}})

\displaystyle:=\bm{F}(\bm{e_{A}},\bm{e_{4}}),

(3.1)

\displaystyle\bm{\underline{\alpha}}(\bm{e_{A}})

\displaystyle:=\bm{F}(\bm{e_{A}},\bm{e_{3}}),

(3.2)

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\,\bm{F}(\bm{e_{3}},\bm{e_{4}}),

(3.3)

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\,\bm{\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}}\bm{F}(\bm{e_{A}},\bm{e_{B}})

(3.4)

with
\bm{A}=\left\{\bm{1},\bm{2}\right\}
, where
\bm{\not{\varepsilon}}
is the standard volume form associated to the metric
\bm{\not{g}}
induced by
\bm{g}
over
\bm{\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}}
. We note that, from (
3.4
), we have
\bm{F}_{\bm{AB}}=\bm{\sigma}\bm{\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}}
. We will refer to
\bm{\alpha},\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
as the
extremal
Maxwell components and to
\bm{\rho},\bm{\sigma}
as the
middle
Maxwell components.

Making use of the formulae in
[
10
, Section 4.6]
, we compute the following identities
9
9

9

In our notation, the Latin capital letters are frame indices, with the first letters of the alphabet running over the horizontal indices only. Note also that one needs to keep track of the order of the indices of the second fundamental forms, which are not in general symmetric tensors.

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{B4}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\not{\nabla}_{A}}\bm{\alpha})_{\bm{B}}+\bm{\zeta_{A}}\bm{\alpha_{B}}-\bm{\chi_{AB}}\bm{\rho}-\bm{{}^{\star}\chi_{BA}}\bm{\sigma}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{B3}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\not{\nabla}_{A}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}})_{\bm{B}}-\bm{\zeta_{A}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{B}}+\bm{\underline{\chi}_{AB}}\bm{\rho}-\bm{{}^{\star}\underline{\chi}_{BA}}\bm{\sigma}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{34}}

\displaystyle=2\bm{\not{\nabla}_{4}}\bm{\rho}-2\,\bm{\underline{\eta}^{A}}\bm{\alpha_{A}}+2\,\bm{\xi^{A}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{A}}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{43}}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\not{\nabla}_{3}}\bm{\rho}-2\,\bm{\eta^{A}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{A}}+2\,\bm{\underline{\xi}^{A}}\bm{\alpha_{A}}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{AB}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\not{\nabla}_{4}}\bm{\sigma})\bm{\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}}+\bm{\underline{\eta}_{A}}\bm{\alpha_{B}}-\bm{\underline{\eta}_{B}}\bm{\alpha_{A}}+\bm{\xi_{A}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{B}}-\bm{\xi_{B}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{A}}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{AB}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\not{\nabla}_{3}}\bm{\sigma})\bm{\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}}+\bm{\eta_{A}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{B}}-\bm{\eta_{B}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{A}}+\bm{\underline{\xi}_{A}}\bm{\alpha_{B}}-\bm{\underline{\xi}_{B}}\bm{\alpha_{A}}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{BC}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\not{\nabla}_{A}}\bm{\sigma})\bm{\not{\varepsilon}_{BC}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\bm{\chi_{AB}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{C}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\bm{\underline{\chi}_{AB}}\bm{\alpha_{C}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\bm{\chi_{AC}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{B}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\bm{\underline{\chi}_{AC}}\bm{\alpha_{B}}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{3A}}

\displaystyle=-(\bm{\not{\nabla}_{4}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}})_{\bm{A}}-\hat{\bm{\omega}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}_{\bm{A}}-2\,\bm{\underline{\eta}_{A}}\bm{\rho}+2\,{}^{\bm{\star}}\bm{\underline{\eta}_{A}}\bm{\sigma}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{A4}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\not{\nabla}_{3}}\bm{\alpha})_{\bm{A}}+\underline{\hat{\bm{\omega}}}\bm{\alpha}_{\bm{A}}-2\,\bm{\eta_{A}}\bm{\rho}-2\,\bm{{}^{\star}\eta_{A}}\bm{\sigma}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{34}}

\displaystyle=2\bm{\not{\nabla}_{A}}\bm{\rho}-\bm{\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{B}}\bm{\alpha_{B}}+\bm{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{B}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}_{B}}\,,

where
⋆
denotes the horizontal Hodge dual as defined in
[
10
, Section 4.3]
. We recall that
\bm{\not{\nabla}}
is defined as the connection induced by
\bm{\nabla}
over
\bm{\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}}
. In general,
\bm{\not{\nabla}}
is not the Levi-Civita connection of
\bm{\not{g}}
. Nonetheless, one has
\bm{\not{\nabla}_{X}\not{g}}=\bm{\not{\nabla}_{X}\not{\varepsilon}}=0
for any
\bm{X}\in\Gamma(T\bm{\mathcal{M}})
. These metric compatibility properties have been used repeatedly in the computations above.

We now assume that
\bm{F}
satisfies the (source-free) Maxwell equations

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla_{I}}\bm{F})_{\bm{JK}}+(\bm{\nabla_{J}}\bm{F})_{\bm{KI}}+(\bm{\nabla_{K}}\bm{F})_{\bm{IJ}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\bm{g^{JK}}(\bm{\nabla_{J}}\bm{F})_{\bm{KI}}

\displaystyle=0

(3.5)

on
\bm{\mathcal{M}}
, with
\bm{I},\bm{J},\bm{K}\in\left\{\bm{1},\bm{2},\bm{3},\bm{4}\right\}
. We can expand the second set of equations in (
3.5
) as

\bm{\not{g}^{AB}}(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{BI}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{4I}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{3I}}=0\,.

(3.6)

Taking
\bm{I}\in\left\{\bm{3},\bm{4}\right\}
in (
3.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bm{\not{g}^{AB}}(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{B4}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{34}}\,,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bm{\not{g}^{AB}}(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{B3}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{43}}\,,

which, noting that
(\bm{\textbf{tr}{}^{\star}\chi})=(\bm{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})
and
(\textbf{tr}\bm{{}^{\star}\underline{\chi}})=(\bm{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})
, can be written as

\displaystyle\bm{\not{\nabla}_{4}}\bm{\rho}+(\bm{\textbf{tr}\chi})\bm{\rho}

\displaystyle=\not{\textbf{div}}\,\bm{\alpha}+(\bm{\zeta}+\bm{\underline{\eta}},\bm{\alpha})-(\bm{\xi},\bm{\underline{\alpha}})-(\bm{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\bm{\not{\nabla}_{3}}\bm{\rho}+(\textbf{tr}\bm{\underline{\chi}})\bm{\rho}

\displaystyle=-\not{\textbf{div}}\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+(\bm{\zeta}-\bm{\eta},\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\bm{\underline{\xi}},\bm{\alpha})+(\bm{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})\bm{\sigma}\,.

(3.8)

We now consider the first set of equations in (
3.5
). Taking
\bm{I}=\bm{A}
,
\bm{J}=\bm{B}
and
\bm{K}\in\left\{\bm{3},\bm{4}\right\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{AB}}+(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{B4}}-(\bm{\nabla_{B}}\bm{F})_{\bm{A4}}\,,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{AB}}+(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{B3}}-(\bm{\nabla_{B}}\bm{F})_{\bm{A3}}\,,

which, noting that
(\bm{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{}^{\star}\chi})=(\textbf{tr}\bm{\chi})
and
(\bm{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{}^{\star}\underline{\chi}})=(\textbf{tr}\bm{\underline{\chi}})
, can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\bm{\not{\nabla}_{4}}\bm{\sigma}+(\textbf{tr}\bm{\chi})\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\bm{\not{\textbf{curl}}}\,\bm{\alpha}-(\bm{\zeta}+\bm{\underline{\eta}})\bm{\wedge}\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\xi}\bm{\wedge}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+(\bm{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\bm{\rho}\,,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\bm{\not{\nabla}_{3}}\bm{\sigma}+(\textbf{tr}\bm{\underline{\chi}})\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\bm{\not{\textbf{curl}}}\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+(\bm{\zeta}-\bm{\eta})\bm{\wedge}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-\bm{\underline{\xi}}\bm{\wedge}\bm{\alpha}-(\bm{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})\bm{\rho}\,.

(3.10)

Finally, we consider the first set of equations in (
3.5
) with
\bm{I}=\bm{4}
,
\bm{J}=\bm{3}
and
\bm{K}=\bm{A}
and the second set of equations in (
3.5
) with
\bm{I}=\bm{A}
to obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{3A}}-(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{34}}+(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{A4}}\,,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bm{\not{g}^{BC}}(\bm{\nabla_{B}}\bm{F})_{\bm{CA}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{A4}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{3A}}\,.

By summing these two equations, and repeating the procedure for the conjugate equations under the exchange of the frame indices
\bm{3}
and
\bm{4}
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bm{\not{g}^{BC}}(\bm{\nabla_{B}}\bm{F})_{\bm{CA}}-(\bm{\nabla_{4}}\bm{F})_{\bm{3A}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{34}}\,,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bm{\not{g}^{BC}}(\bm{\nabla_{B}}\bm{F})_{\bm{CA}}+(\bm{\nabla_{3}}\bm{F})_{\bm{A4}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\bm{\nabla_{A}}\bm{F})_{\bm{34}}\,,

which can be written as

\displaystyle\bm{\not{\nabla}_{4}}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\bm{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-\hat{\bm{\chi}}{}^{\bm{\sharp}}\bm{\cdot}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\bm{\omega}}\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\bm{\underline{\chi}}}{}^{\bm{\sharp}}\bm{\cdot}\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\not{\nabla}}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\bm{\star}}\bm{\not{\nabla}}\bm{\sigma}-2\,\bm{\underline{\eta}}\,\bm{\rho}+2\,{}^{\bm{\star}}\bm{\underline{\eta}}\,\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\bm{\not{\nabla}_{3}}\bm{\alpha}+\bm{\underline{\chi}}^{\bm{\sharp_{2}}}\bm{\cdot}\bm{\alpha}-\hat{\bm{\underline{\chi}}}{}^{\bm{\sharp}}\bm{\cdot}\bm{\alpha}+\underline{\hat{\bm{\omega}}}\,\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\hat{\bm{\chi}}{}^{\bm{\sharp}}\bm{\cdot}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\bm{\not{\nabla}}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\bm{\star}}\bm{\not{\nabla}}\bm{\sigma}+2\,\bm{\eta}\,\bm{\rho}+2\,{}^{\bm{\star}}\bm{\eta}\,\bm{\sigma}\,.

(3.12)

The equations (
3.7
)–(
3.12
) constitute the system of Maxwell equations on
(\bm{\mathcal{M}},\bm{g})
relative to
\bm{\mathcal{N}}
.

3.2
The equations on the Kerr spacetime relative to the algebraically special frame

In this section, we specialise the general system of Maxwell equations (
3.7
)–(
3.12
) derived in Section
3.1
by identifying
(\bm{\mathcal{M}},\bm{g})
with the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
and
\bm{\mathcal{N}}
with the algebraically special frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
of
(\mathcal{M},g)
(see Section
2
). In the notation, the unknowns in the equations remain boldfaced, whereas the background quantities are now unbolded.

The equations (
3.7
)–(
3.12
) take the form

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\sigma}+(\tr\underline{\chi})\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\bm{\rho}\,,

(3.13)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\rho}+(\tr\underline{\chi})\bm{\rho}

\displaystyle=-\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\sigma}+(\tr\chi)\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\curlsl\bm{\alpha}-(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge\bm{\alpha}+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\bm{\rho}\,,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\rho}+(\tr\chi)\bm{\rho}

\displaystyle=\divsl\bm{\alpha}+(\eta+\underline{\eta},\bm{\alpha})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.16)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\alpha}+\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}\bm{\sigma}+2\,\eta\,\bm{\rho}+2\,{}^{\star}\eta\,\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.17)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\omega}\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-\not{\nabla}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}\bm{\sigma}-2\,\underline{\eta}\,\bm{\rho}+2\,{}^{\star}\underline{\eta}\,\bm{\sigma}\,.

(3.18)

The equations (
3.13
)–(
3.18
) constitute the system of Maxwell equations on
(\mathcal{M},g)
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
. For future convenience, we note that, in view of the identities of Section
2.2.4
, the system can be rewritten in the form

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=-\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\Sigma\bm{\rho}\,,

(3.19)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=-\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta},\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\Sigma\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.20)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=-\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\Sigma\bm{\rho}\,,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\Sigma\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.22)

\displaystyle\Sigma(\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\alpha}+\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})+(\eta-\underline{\eta})\Sigma\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\Sigma\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.23)

\displaystyle\Sigma(\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\omega}\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=-\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})+(\eta-\underline{\eta})\Sigma\bm{\rho}-{}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\Sigma\bm{\sigma}\,.

(3.24)

3.2.1
The Teukolsky equations

From the Maxwell equations (
3.13
)–(
3.18
), one can show that the extremal Maxwell components
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
each satisfy a decoupled wave equation:

Proposition 3.1

(Tensorial Teukolsky equations)
.

We have the identities

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Sigma\left(\not{\nabla}_{4}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}\right)\!\left(\not{\nabla}_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star}\right)\bm{\alpha}\\
&\qquad-(\not{\nabla}+2\eta)\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\eta)\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})=0\,,\end{split}

(3.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(\not{\nabla}_{3}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star}\right)\!\left(\not{\nabla}_{4}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}+\hat{\omega}\right)\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\\
&\qquad-(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}=0\,.\end{split}

(3.26)

Equations (
3.25
)–(
3.26
) are henceforth known as the (tensorial) Teukolsky equations. We direct the reader to Appendix
A.2
for a detailed derivation.
10
10

10

As in this paper we are not concerned with an in-depth analysis of the Teukolsky equations, either in tensorial or spinorial form, this derivation is included only as a reference for the reader.
We remark that the decoupling of the extremal Maxwell components is intimately tied to the fact that the frame components of the Maxwell field (i.e. the unknowns of the Maxwell system) are defined relative to the algebraically special frame.

In the above,
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
are one-forms over the horizontal distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}}
, hence (
3.25
)–(
3.26
) are equations for smooth sections
\Gamma((\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}})^{\star})
. In contrast, the original derivation of the Teukolsky equations, due to Teukolsky
[
40
]
, in the Newman–Penrose formalism
[
31
]
yielded equations

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg[\Box_{g}&+\frac{2(\pm 1)}{\Sigma^{2}}(r-M)\partial_{r}+\frac{2(\pm 1)}{\Sigma^{2}}\left(\frac{a(r-M)}{\Delta}+i\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}\right)\partial_{\phi}\\
&+\frac{2(\pm 1)}{\Sigma^{2}}\left(\frac{M(r^{2}-a^{2})}{\Delta}-r-ia\cos\theta\right)\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\Sigma^{2}}\left(\pm 1-\cot^{2}\theta\right)\bigg]\upalpha^{[\pm 1]}=0\end{split}

(3.27)

for complex-valued functions
\upalpha^{[{\pm 1}]}
such that
\Delta^{\pm 1}\upalpha^{[{\pm 1}]}
are smooth,
(\pm 1)-
spin-weighted functions on
\mathcal{M}
. We denote this function space by
\mathscr{S}^{[\pm 1]}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, and direct the reader to e.g.
[
35
, Section 3.1.1]
for a precise definition. In (
3.27
),
\Box_{g}
denotes the covariant wave operator (i.e. the Laplace–Beltrami operator) for scalar functions on the Kerr manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
.

Let

\kappa(r,\theta):=r-ia\cos\theta

be a smooth complex-valued function on
\mathcal{M}
. The tensorial and spin-weighted quantities can be suitably identified:

Lemma 3.2
.

The maps
\mathrm{i}^{[\pm 1]}\colon\Gamma((\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}})^{\star})\to\mathscr{S}^{[\pm 1]}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{\alpha}&\xmapsto{\mathrm{i}^{[+1]}}\Delta\upalpha^{[{+1}]}:=-\frac{\Sigma}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\bm{\alpha}(e_{1}^{\rm as})+i\bm{\alpha}(e_{2}^{\rm as})\right],\\
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}&\xmapsto{\mathrm{i}^{[-1]}}\Delta^{-1}\upalpha^{[{-1}]}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\Sigma}\kappa^{2}\left[\bm{\underline{\alpha}}(e_{1}^{\rm as})-i\bm{\underline{\alpha}}(e_{2}^{\rm as})\right],\end{split}

(3.28)

where
(e_{1}^{\rm as},e_{2}^{\rm as})
are chosen to be given by the explicit formula (
2.13
), define an isomorphism between
\Gamma((\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}})^{\star})
and
\mathscr{S}^{[\pm 1]}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
with the properties

\displaystyle|\Delta\upalpha^{[{+1}]}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lvert\Sigma\bm{\alpha}\rvert_{\not{g}}^{2},

\displaystyle|(r^{2}+a^{2})^{1/2}\Delta^{-1}\upalpha^{[{-1}]}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lvert(r^{2}+a^{2})^{1/2}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\rvert_{\not{g}}^{2}.

The isomorphisms
\mathrm{i}^{[\pm 1]}
descend to isomorphisms between the space of solutions to the tensorial Teukolsky equations (
3.25
)–(
3.26
) and, respectively, the space of solutions to the
(\pm 1)
-spin-weighted Teukolsky equations (
3.27
).

Proof.

It is not hard to check that, for
\bm{\alpha},\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\in\Gamma((\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\rm as}})^{\star})
, the expressions

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}(e_{1}^{\rm as})+i\bm{\alpha}(e_{2}^{\rm as}),

\displaystyle\bm{\underline{\alpha}}(e_{1}^{\rm as})-i\bm{\underline{\alpha}}(e_{2}^{\rm as})

with
(e_{1}^{\rm as},e_{2}^{\rm as})
given in (
2.13
) indeed define, respectively, smooth
(\pm 1)
-spin-weighted functions on
\mathcal{M}
as defined in e.g.
[
35
, Section 3.1.1]
. Furthermore, the maps (
3.28
) are clearly invertible:

\displaystyle\upalpha^{[{+1}]}\equiv 0\Leftrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}\upalpha^{[{+1}]}\equiv 0\equiv\bm{\alpha}(e_{1}^{\rm as})\\
\operatorname{Im}\upalpha^{[{+1}]}\equiv 0\equiv\bm{\alpha}(e_{2}^{\rm as})\end{array}\right\}\Leftrightarrow\bm{\alpha}\equiv 0,

and similarly for the spin
-1
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
case.
∎

3.2.2
The modified Maxwell equations

We introduce the modified quantities

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\rho}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma[(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})^{2}]\,\Sigma\bm{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\tr\underline{\chi})\,\Sigma\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\tr\underline{\chi})\,\Sigma\bm{\rho}+\frac{1}{4}\Sigma[(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})^{2}]\,\Sigma\bm{\sigma}\,,

(3.30)

which, in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, read

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\rho}}

\displaystyle=(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\,\bm{\rho}-2ar\cos\theta\,\bm{\sigma}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=2ar\cos\theta\,\bm{\rho}+(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\,\bm{\sigma}\,.

We note the identity

\widehat{\bm{\rho}}^{2}+\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}^{2}=(\Sigma\bm{\rho})^{2}+(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})^{2}\,.

(3.31)

In this section, we will show:

Proposition 3.3

(Modified Maxwell equations)
.

We have the identities

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\tr\underline{\chi})\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{2}[(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})^{2}]\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\,,

(3.32)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{2}[(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})^{2}]\,\divsl\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\tr\underline{\chi})\curlsl\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\,,

(3.33)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\tr\underline{\chi})\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma[(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})^{2}]\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})\,,

(3.34)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{4}\Sigma[(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})^{2}]\,\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\tr\underline{\chi})\,\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})\,,

(3.35)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\tr\underline{\chi})\,(\mathfrak{T}(\bm{\alpha})-\underline{\mathfrak{T}}(\bm{\underline{\alpha}}))-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma[(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})^{2}]\,({}^{\star}\mathfrak{T}(\bm{\alpha})+{}^{\star}\underline{\mathfrak{T}}(\bm{\underline{\alpha}}))\,,

(3.36)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\Sigma[(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})^{2}]\,(\mathfrak{T}(\bm{\alpha})-\underline{\mathfrak{T}}(\bm{\underline{\alpha}}))-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\tr\underline{\chi})\,({}^{\star}\mathfrak{T}(\bm{\alpha})+{}^{\star}\underline{\mathfrak{T}}(\bm{\underline{\alpha}}))\,,

(3.37)

where we have set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}(\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\,\Sigma(\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\alpha}+\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\alpha})\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathfrak{T}}(\bm{\underline{\alpha}})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\,\Sigma(\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\omega}\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\,.

(3.38)

The equations (
3.32
)–(
3.37
) constitute the
modified system of Maxwell equations
. Crucially, in this system, the equations (
3.33
), (
3.35
) and (
3.37
) for
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
are
decoupled
from the equations (
3.32
), (
3.34
) and (
3.36
) for
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
.

Remark 3.4
.

The modified system of Maxwell equations (
3.32
)–(
3.37
) has an analogue in the language of the spin-weighted functions introduced in the previous section. Indeed, with
\upalpha^{[{\pm 1}]}
as defined in (
3.28
), where we have fixed
(e_{1}^{\rm as},e_{2}^{\rm as})
to be given by (
2.13
), and letting

\displaystyle\upupsilon^{[{0}]}:=-\widehat{\bm{\rho}}+i\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}

(3.39)

be a smooth complex-valued function on
\mathcal{M}
, the Maxwell equations can be written in the form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({e_{1}^{\rm as}}+i{e_{2}^{\rm as}})\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{3}}{2\Sigma}e_{3}^{\rm as}\left(\kappa^{-1}\Delta\upalpha^{[{+1}]}\right),

(3.40)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({e_{1}^{\rm as}}-i{e_{2}^{\rm as}})\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa\Sigma}{2\Delta}e_{4}^{\rm as}\left(\kappa^{-1}\upalpha^{[{-1}]}\right),

(3.41)

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}e_{4}^{\rm as}\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\kappa({e_{1}^{\rm as}}-i{e_{2}^{\rm as}})+\cot\theta\right)\left(\kappa^{-1}\upalpha^{[{+1}]}\right),

(3.42)

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta}{2\Sigma}e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2\sqrt{2}\Sigma}\left(\overline{\kappa}({e_{1}^{\rm as}}+i{e_{2}^{\rm as}})+\cot\theta\right)\left(\kappa^{-1}\upalpha^{[{-1}]}\right),

(3.43)

which can be found from the usual Newman–Penrose formalism for the Kerr metric, see e.g. the classical textbook
[
14
, Section 69, eq. (11)]
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

We start by deriving the equations (
3.32
)–(
3.33
) and (
3.34
)–(
3.35
). We compute

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}=

\displaystyle\,-(2a\cos\theta+2ar\cos\theta(\tr\underline{\chi})+(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}))\bm{\rho}

\displaystyle+(-2r+2ar\cos\theta(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})-(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)(\tr\underline{\chi}))\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle-2ar\cos\theta\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}

and

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}=

\displaystyle\,(2a\cos\theta+2ar\cos\theta(\tr\underline{\chi})+(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}))\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle+(-2r+2ar\cos\theta(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})-(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)(\tr\underline{\chi}))\bm{\rho}

\displaystyle+2ar\cos\theta\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\,,

where we used the equations (
3.13
)–(
3.14
). Using Section
2.2.4
, both the brackets multiplying
\bm{\rho}
and
\bm{\sigma}
can be checked to identically vanish, i.e.

\displaystyle 2a\cos\theta+2ar\cos\theta(\tr\underline{\chi})+(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle-2r+2ar\cos\theta(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})-(r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)(\tr\underline{\chi})

\displaystyle=0\,.

Similarly, we compute

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}=
