on the right hand side. These terms will be treated in the sequel (see Sections
6.3
–
6.4
).

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
\epsilon>0
.
Under the assumptions of Lemma
6.6
, there exists a uniform constant
C=C(M,p_{\alpha},\epsilon)>0
such that the following estimates hold.

•

L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau})
-boundedness: we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{-2}(|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]|^{2}+|\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]|^{2})d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}+C\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\left(\mathbb{D}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]](0)+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]](\tau)+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]](\tau)\right)

for all
\tau\geq 0
.

•

L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau})
-decay: there exist finite
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, which can be read off from the seed initial data, such that the normalised solution
\widetilde{\bm{\mathfrak{S}}}=\bm{\mathfrak{S}}-\bm{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{-2}(|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}[\widetilde{\bm{\mathfrak{S}}}]|^{2}+|\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}[\widetilde{\bm{\mathfrak{S}}}]|^{2})d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}+C\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\left(\frac{\mathbb{D}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]](0)}{\tau^{p_{\alpha}}}+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]](\tau)+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]](\tau)\right)

for all
\tau\geq 0
.

Proof.

The proof can be divided into two steps: in the first step we control the relevant fluxes for large
r\geq R\gg r_{+}
, whereas in the second step we control the relevant fluxes in the bounded-
r
region up to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. We present the proof of the estimates for
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
. The case of
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
follows by the same arguments.

Step 1: the large-
r
region.
We integrate the identity

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}(r^{-1}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2})+\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}\Sigma}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{r}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}

over the hypersurface
\Sigma_{\tau}
and obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}\Sigma}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega dr

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\not{\nabla}_{4}\left(\frac{1}{r}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}\right)d\Omega dr+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\frac{2}{r}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{R}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,R}}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\left(\tilde{X}-\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)\left(\frac{1}{r}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}\right)d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\frac{2}{r}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega dr\,,

where we introduced the vector field
\tilde{X}
defined in (
2.8
) and dropped
\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}-\frac{1}{r}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega\leq 0
. By a Cauchy–Schwarz inequality applied to the last two terms, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{-2}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,R}}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\Big|\left(\tilde{X}-\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)(r^{-1})\Big||\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega dr

(6.10)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\left[|\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}+|\left(\tilde{X}-\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}\right]d\Omega dr.

By direct inspection of definition (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle|\left(\tilde{X}-\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)(r^{-1})|\lesssim r^{-3}\,,

and therefore the second term on the right hand side of (
6.10
) can be absorbed into the left hand side for
R
sufficiently large, which we fix accordingly. Then, using the modified Maxwell equations, we observe

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\left[|\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}+|\left(\tilde{X}-\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}\right]d\Omega dr\lesssim\mathbb{E}^{1}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}](\tau)+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}](\tau).

Putting everything together, we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{-2}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,R}}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}](\tau)+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}](\tau)\,.

Step 2: the bounded-
r
region.

Let

\displaystyle f(\tau,r):=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega

and note the Poincaré inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega\lesssim\left|f(\tau,r)\right|^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}r^{2}|{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega\,.

We can integrate the above inequality over the hypersurface
\Sigma_{\tau}
and obtain (note that, since we are integrating in a bounded-
r
region, the
r
-weights in the following estimates are in fact irrelevant)

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}r^{-2}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{r_{+}}^{R}r^{-2}\left|f(\tau,r)\right|^{2}dr+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}|{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{r_{+}}^{R}r^{-2}\left|f(\tau,r)\right|^{2}dr+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}](\tau)+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}](\tau)\,,

(6.11)

where we have used the modified Maxwell equations in the second inequality. We remark that the inequality

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}|{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega dr\lesssim\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}\left(\left|\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\right|^{2}+\left|\Delta\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\right|^{2}+\left|\not{\nabla}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\right|^{2}\right)d\Omega dr

and the structure of the equations are, in particular, used to conclude that inequality (
6.11
) can in fact be written in terms of degenerate-at-
\mathcal{H}^{+}
energy norms of
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
. For the first term in (
6.11
), we observe

\displaystyle\int_{r_{+}}^{R}r^{-2}\left|f(\tau,r)\right|^{2}dr\leq\sup_{r\in[r_{+}R]}\left|f(\tau,r)\right|^{2}\int_{r_{+}}^{R}r^{-2}dr\leq\frac{1}{r_{+}}\sup_{r\in[r_{+}R]}\left|f(\tau,r)\right|^{2}.

We then apply the fundamental theorem of calculus and obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[r_{+},R]}\left|f(\tau,r)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|f(\tau,r_{+})|^{2}+\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{R}\partial_{r}f(\tau,r)dr\Bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\epsilon}|f(\tau,r_{+})|^{2}+\Bigg|\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}\left(\left|\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\right|^{2}+\left|\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\right|^{2}+\left|\not{\nabla}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\right|^{2}\right)d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\epsilon}\left|f(\tau,r_{+})\right|^{2}+\Bigg|\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}](\tau)+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}](\tau)

for any
\epsilon>0
, where we used the modified Maxwell equations in the last inequality. We remark that the equations do not allow to estimate the second term in the last inequality in terms of the degenerate-at-
\mathcal{H}^{+}
energy norm
\mathbb{E}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}](\tau)
.

Putting everything together, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}r^{-2}|\widehat{\bm{\rho}}|^{2}d\Omega dr

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\epsilon}\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega\Bigg|^{2}+\Bigg|\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}](\tau)+\mathbb{E}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}](\tau)\,.

By combining with the previous step and with Lemma
6.6
, we conclude the proof of the proposition.
∎

6.2
Control over higher order fluxes

Control over first (and higher) order energy fluxes of the modified middle Maxwell components
\widehat{\bm{\rho}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}
can be immediately read off from the modified Maxwell equations (
3.32
)–(
3.37
).

Proposition 6.10
.

Fix
M>0
and let
J\geq 0
. Then, there exists a uniform constant
C=C(M,J)>0
such that, for any
|a|\leq M
and any solution

\bm{\mathfrak{S}}=(\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}],\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}],\widehat{\bm{\rho}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}],\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}])

to the modified Maxwell equations (
3.32
)–(
3.37
), the energy estimates

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbb{E}}^{J}\big[\widehat{\bm{\rho}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)+\dot{\mathbb{E}}^{J}\big[\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)

\displaystyle\leq C\left({\mathbb{E}}^{\max\{J,1\}}\big[r^{2}{\bm{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)+{\mathbb{E}}^{\max\{J,1\}}\big[r{\bm{\underline{\alpha}}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)\right)\,,

\displaystyle\dot{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}^{J}\big[\widehat{\bm{\rho}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)+\dot{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}^{J}\big[\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{\max\{J,1\}}\big[r^{2}{\bm{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)+{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}^{\max\{J,1\}}\big[r{\bm{\underline{\alpha}}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)\right)

hold for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.

6.3
Proof of Theorem
6.1

By combining the results of Sections
6.1
–
6.2
, one obtains:

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

By the modified Maxwell equations (
3.32
)–(
3.33
), we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}+\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}\lesssim\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\left(|\not{\nabla}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}|^{2}+|\bm{\underline{\alpha}}|^{2}\right)d\Omega dr\lesssim\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{1}[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}](\tau)\,,

where the implicit constants are independent of
\epsilon
. By combining the above inequality with Propositions
6.9
and
6.10
, we conclude the proof of the theorem.
∎

We recall that the reader interested only in the analysis for the full sub-extremal
|a|<M
case can now skip ahead to Section
7
. The reader interested also in the nearly extremal (
|a|\approx M
) and exactly extremal (
|a|=M
) cases should continue to the next sections.

6.4
Proof of Theorem
6.2

The aim of this section is to prove Theorem
6.2
by combining Propositions
6.9
and
6.10
. This requires one to control the first two terms on the right hand side of the estimates in Proposition
6.9
in terms of
degenerate
-at-
\mathcal{H}^{+}
energy norms of the extremal Maxwell components
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
.

To this end, we recall the notation introduced in Sections
3.2
and
4.1
and the quantities

\upupsilon^{[{0}]}=-\widehat{\bm{\rho}}+i\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\upphi}^{[{+1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle=\Delta\upalpha^{[{+1}]}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Sigma\bm{\alpha}(e_{1}^{\rm as}+ie_{2}^{\rm as})\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Delta^{-1}\upalpha^{[{-1}]}=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}\Sigma}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{1/2}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}(e_{1}^{\rm as}-ie_{2}^{\rm as})\,,

\displaystyle\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}\Sigma e_{4}^{\rm as}(\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0})\,,

\displaystyle\Phi^{[{+1}]}

\displaystyle=(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}^{\rm as}(\upphi^{[{+1}]}_{0})\,.

We will also employ the scalar function

\displaystyle w(r):=\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}\,.

It will be useful to keep in mind Lemma
3.2
and Remark
3.4
(see also Appendix
A.4
) throughout this section.

The core of the section is to derive a suitable estimate for the quantity

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\rho}}\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}+\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}\,d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}=\Bigg|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}\big)d\Omega dr\Bigg|^{2}

(6.12)

with
\epsilon>0
to be fixed.

6.4.1
Warm-up: from Schwarzschild to extremal Reissner–Nordström

To illustrate our approach, we first consider the Schwarzschild
|a|=0
case. The following proposition and its proof will serve as a guide for the more intricate calculations in the
|a|\neq 0
case.

Proposition 6.11

(An identity on Schwarzschild)
.

Fix
M>0
and let
|a|=0
. For
L\geq|s|
, let

\displaystyle S_{L}^{[s]}(\theta,\phi):=\sum_{\max\{|m|,|s|\}\leq L}S_{m,L}^{[s]}(\theta)e^{im\phi}

be the
s
spin-weighted spherical harmonics, which form a complete orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\sin\theta d\theta)
, see for instance
[
35
, Section 3.1.2]
for a rigorous definition. Then, by writing

\displaystyle\upupsilon^{[{0}]}_{L}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\upupsilon^{[{0}]}S_{L}^{[0]}d\Omega\,,\quad L\geq 0\,,\qquad\qquad\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Phi^{[{-1}]}S_{L}^{[-1]}d\Omega\,,\quad L\geq 1\,,

we have the pointwise identity

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}_{L}\big)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2r^{2}}}\begin{cases}0\,,&L=0\\
\frac{e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L}\big)}{\sqrt{L(L+1)}}\,,&L\geq 1\end{cases}\,.

(6.13)

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps.

Step 1: an identity from the Teukolsky equation
. From the spin
-1
Teukolsky equation (
3.27
), using (
4.1.1
), we deduce the constraint identity

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\Phi^{[{-1}]}\big)=-(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}+1)\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\,,

where
\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}
is the
(-1)
-spin-weighted Laplacian. See already Lemma
6.12
below for an explicit formula and more detail regarding the derivation of this identity.

Step 2: inverting an angular operator
. The spin-weighted spherical harmonics are eigenfunctions for the angular operator
\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}+1
, and

\displaystyle\left(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}+1\right)S_{L}^{[-1]}(\theta,\phi)=L(L+1)S_{L}^{[-1]}(\theta,\phi)\,.

Since
L\geq 1
, we have
L(L+1)\geq 2
, and hence we can define the inverse operator
\left(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}+1\right)^{-1}
on
L^{2}(\sin\theta d\theta)
. Indeed, we can explicitly write

\displaystyle\left(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}+1\right)^{-1}S_{L}^{[-1]}(\theta,\phi)=\frac{1}{L(L+1)}S_{L}^{[-1]}(\theta,\phi)\,.

Step 3: an identity from the Maxwell equations
. From the Maxwell equation (
3.43
), we have

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\,\upupsilon^{[{0}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2r^{2}}}\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\phi}+\cot\theta\right)\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\,.

Conclusion
. By combining the previous steps, we have

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\,\upupsilon^{[{0}]}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2r^{2}}}\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\phi}+\cot\theta\right)(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}+1)^{-1}e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\Phi^{[{-1}]}\big)\,.

The conclusion follows by a computation, and using the orthogonality of the spin-weighted spherical harmonics. It will be useful to use the identity

\displaystyle-\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\phi}+\cot\theta\right)S_{L}^{[-1]}(\theta,\phi)=\sqrt{L(L+1)}S_{L}^{[0]}(\theta,\phi),\quad L\geq 1,

as the differential operator on the left hand side is the spin-raising operator.
∎

Proposition
6.11
does
not
exploit the sub-extremality, or positive surface gravity, of the Schwarzschild solution. In fact, the pointwise identity (
6.13
) continues to hold if one replaces the Schwarzschild solution
(\mathcal{M},g_{0,M})
with any Reissner–Nordström solution
(\mathcal{M},g_{e,M})
with
|e|\leq M
. Indeed, the identities

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2r^{2}}}\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\phi}+\cot\theta\right)\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\,,

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{\theta}-\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\phi}\right)\upupsilon^{[{0}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2r^{2}}}\Phi^{[{-1}]}

still hold for the Maxwell equations on Reissner–Nordström spacetimes, where one can check that equations (
3.40
)–(
3.43
) hold on Reissner–Nordström after replacing
\Delta
with
r^{2}-2Mr+e^{2}
and then setting
|a|=0
(see
[
14
, Chapter 44, eq. (200)–(203)]
). For
|e|=M
, We remark that the relation
e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}_{L}\big)\sim_{r,L}e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L}\big)
for
L\geq 1
implies that
\upupsilon^{[{0}]}
inherits the dynamics of the transversal derivatives of
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and in particular the conservation laws for transversal derivatives of
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
which can be derived for example by adapting
[
5
, Section 6]
. This would provide information on the nature of the Maxwell horizon hair formed in the evolution of the Maxwell equations on extremal Reissner--Nordström spacetimes
12
12

12

We again emphasize that the evolution problem for the Maxwell equations on extremal Reissner–Nordström black holes is distinct from the evolution problem for the (linearised) Einstein–Maxwell system around extremal Reissner–Nordström. It is the latter (not the former) which is the object of study in
[
5
]
.
.

6.4.2
Three key lemmas

We now consider the
|a|\neq 0
case. The aim of the section is to generalise the three steps in the proof of Proposition
6.11
to the full
|a|\leq M
range. First, we use the spin
-1
Teukolsky equation (
3.27
) to deduce a useful identity for
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
and
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}
. In the remainder of the section, we adopt the notation
f^{\prime}=\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}f
for any scalar function
f
.

Lemma 6.12
.

Fix
M>0
and let
|a|\leq M
. For
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
and
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}
as above, we have the pointwise identity

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle=-e_{3}^{\rm as}\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\left[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\left(\frac{\Sigma e_{4}^{\rm as}}{4(r^{2}+a^{2})}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+\frac{\Delta e_{3}^{\rm as}}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}-\frac{w^{\prime}}{4w}\right)+a(Z+i\cos\theta)\right]\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{1}{4}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta e_{3}^{\rm as}\left(\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}e_{3}^{\rm as}\right)+\frac{a\Sigma}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Ze_{3}^{\rm as}+ia\cos\theta e_{3}^{\rm as}\right)\left(\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+aw\left[a-\frac{a^{2}r\sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})}{M}Z\right]\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{a^{2}}{Mr_{+}}\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\left[Z+i\cos\theta-\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{2Mr_{+}}\left(1-\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}Z\right)\right]Z\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\,,

(6.14)

where, letting
\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}
denote the
-1
spin-weighted spherical Laplacian

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left(\sin\theta\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\right)-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}Z^{2}-2i(-1)\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}Z+(-1)^{2}\cot^{2}\theta\,,

(6.15)

we write
\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}
for the related operator

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}:=\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}+1+\frac{a^{2}}{Mr_{+}}\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{4Mr_{+}}\sin^{2}\theta\right)Z^{2}+\frac{a}{Mr_{+}}\left(r_{+}+ia\cos\theta\right)Z\,.

(6.16)

Remark 6.13
.

We note that, schematically, identity (
6.14
) has the form

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}=-e_{3}^{\rm as}\Phi^{[{-1}]}+\dots\,,

where the ellipses represent either terms in which
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}
and its
(e_{3}^{\rm as},e_{2}^{\rm as},e_{1}^{\rm as})
-derivatives are multiplied by functions of
(r,\theta)
which, if
|a|=M
, vanish at least linearly as
r\to r_{+}
, or terms proportional to
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
and its
((r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}\Delta e_{3}^{\rm as},e_{2}^{\rm as},e_{1}^{\rm as})
-derivatives.

Proof of Lemma
6.12
.

As a preliminary step, we rewrite
T
-derivatives of
\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}
in terms of the vector fields
e_{3}^{\rm as}
and
e_{4}^{\rm as}
, or, to make contact with
[
35
]
, in terms of
\underline{L}
and
L
(recall definition (
2.14
)). Making use of (
4.1.1
), we have

\displaystyle T\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\underline{L}+L}{2}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\right)\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\right)\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}w\Phi^{[{-1}]}\,.

(6.17)

We can repeat this calculation to obtain a formula for
T^{2}
-derivatives as follows

\displaystyle T^{2}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}T\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}ZT\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}wT\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}w\Phi^{[{-1}]}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)+\frac{1}{2}w\left(\frac{L+\underline{L}}{2}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\right)\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\underline{L}\underline{L}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\frac{1}{4}\left(w\underline{L}\Phi^{[{-1}]}-w^{\prime}\Phi^{[{-1}]}\right)-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\left(\underline{L}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+rw\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{aw}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}Z\Phi^{[{-1}]}+\frac{a^{2}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}Z^{2}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\frac{1}{4}w(L+\underline{L})\Phi^{[{-1}]}-\frac{aw}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}Z\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}wL\Phi^{[{-1}]}-\frac{aw}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\Phi^{[{-1}]}+\frac{a^{2}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}Z^{2}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}w\underline{L}\Phi^{[{-1}]}-\frac{1}{4}w^{\prime}\Phi^{[{-1}]}+\frac{1}{4}\underline{L}\underline{L}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\underline{L}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}-\frac{awr}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}.

(6.18)

We now consider the spin
-1
Teukolsky equation (
3.27
). From (
4.1.1
), we deduce that it can be written as the constraint equation, cf.
[
35
, Equation (3.19)]
,

\displaystyle\underline{L}\Phi^{[{-1}]}=-(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}_{T}+1)\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}-\frac{2ar}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+a^{2}w\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0},

(6.19)

where, following
[
35
, Equation (3.13)]
, we have defined

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}_{T}=\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta TT-2aTZ-2ia\cos\theta T.

(6.20)

Using the computations in (
6.17
)–(
6.18
), we obtain that the angular operator (
6.20
) acts on
\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}
via

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}-\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}_{T}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle\quad=\left(a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta TT+2aTZ+2ia\cos\theta T\right)\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle\quad=w\left[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\left(\frac{1}{4}L-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}-\frac{w^{\prime}}{4w}\right)+a(Z+i\cos\theta)\right]\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-\frac{2a^{2}}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\left[\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}\sin^{2}\theta\right)Z+i\cos\theta\right]Z\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\left(\frac{1}{4}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\underline{L}+\frac{a\Sigma}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+ia\cos\theta\right)\underline{L}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}-\frac{a^{3}rw\sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}.

We can plug the above into (
6.19
) to obtain

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\left[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\left(\frac{1}{4}L-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}-\frac{w^{\prime}}{4w}\right)+a(Z+i\cos\theta)\right]\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{1}{4}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta e_{3}^{\rm as}\underline{L}+\frac{a\Sigma}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Ze_{3}^{\rm as}+ia\cos\theta e_{3}^{\rm as}\right)\left(\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)+a^{2}w\left[1-\frac{ar\sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\right]\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left[\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[{-1}]}+\frac{2a^{2}}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}\sin^{2}\theta\right)Z^{2}+\frac{2a(r+ia\cos\theta)}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\right]\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}.

Finally, we add and subtract the last line with the differential operator evaluated at
r=r_{+}
.
∎

Second, we show that the operator on the left hand side of (
6.14
) is invertible for the full
|a|\leq M
range.

Lemma 6.14
.

Fix
M>0
and let
|a|\leq M
. Then, the operator
\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\colon L^{2}(d\Omega)\to L^{2}(d\Omega)
is invertible and its norm is bounded in terms of an explicitly computable constant that depends only on
M
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of the analogous statement for the spin-weighted Laplacian
\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[s]}
, see e.g.
[
35
, Section 3.1.2]
. We apply Sturm–Liouville theory to deduce that the operator
\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}
admits a countable family of eigenfunctions of the form
e^{im\tilde{\phi}{}^{*}}\tilde{S}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}(\theta)
, normalized to have unit
L^{2}(d\Omega)
-norm, with corresponding (complex) eigenvalues
\tilde{\lambda}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}
, with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq\max\{|m|,1\}}
being our choice of parameter to index the family. To show the desired invertibility, it is enough to show that
|\tilde{\lambda}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}|
is bounded below uniformly in
(m,\ell)
and with respect to the black hole parameters.

For
m=0
or
|a|=0
, we have
\tilde{\lambda}_{0\ell}^{[-1]}=\ell(\ell+1)\geq 2>0
and
\tilde{S}_{0\ell}^{[-1]}(\theta)=S_{0\ell}^{[-1]}(\theta)
are the usual spin-weighted spherical harmonics. For
|a|\neq 0
and
m\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\tilde{\lambda}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{M}m,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\tilde{\lambda}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left(|\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\Xi}|^{2}+\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{m}|\tilde{\Xi}|^{2}\right)\sin\theta d\theta,

where
\tilde{\Xi}=c\tilde{S}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}(\theta)
for some constant
c
chosen so that the normalization condition
\int_{0}^{\pi}|\tilde{\Xi}|^{2}\sin\theta d\theta=1
holds, and where

\displaystyle\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{m}=\frac{(m\cos\theta-1)^{2}}{\sin^{2}\theta}+\frac{1}{4}\sin^{2}\theta m^{2}-\frac{a^{2}}{Mr_{+}}m\cos\theta+\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{Mr_{+}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{4}\sin^{2}\theta\right)m^{2}.

We divide the analysis into three cases:

•

Case
|m|\geq 3
: We have

\displaystyle\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{m}(\theta)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\theta\in(0,\pi)}\left(\frac{(m\cos\theta-1)^{2}}{\sin^{2}\theta}+\frac{1}{4}\sin^{2}\theta m^{2}-\frac{a^{2}}{Mr_{+}}m\cos\theta\right)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\theta\in(0,\pi/2)}\left(\frac{(m\cos\theta-1)^{2}}{\sin^{2}\theta}+\frac{1}{4}\sin^{2}\theta m^{2}-\frac{a^{2}}{Mr_{+}}|m|\cos\theta\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}

and hence
\operatorname{Re}\tilde{\lambda}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}\geq\frac{1}{2}>0
.

•

Case
|m|=1,2
and
|a|\geq\frac{1}{2}M
: We have
|\operatorname{Im}\tilde{\lambda}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

•

Case
|m|=1,2
and
|a|\leq\frac{1}{2}M
: We obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{\pm 1}(\theta)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\theta\in(0,\pi),|m|=1}\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{m}(\theta)=\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{1}(0)=1-\frac{2a^{2}}{Mr_{+}}\geq\frac{1}{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{\pm 2}(\theta)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\theta\in(0,\pi),|m|=2,|a|\leq\frac{1}{2}M}\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{m}(\theta)=\inf_{\theta\in(0,\pi/2),|a|=\frac{1}{2}M}\tilde{V}^{\rm ang}_{2}(\theta)\geq 2

by an explicit computation, and thus
\operatorname{Re}\tilde{\lambda}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}\geq\frac{1}{2}>0
.

We deduce that, for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq\max\{|m|,1\}}
and
a\in[0,M]
, we have the uniform bound
|\tilde{\lambda}_{m\ell}^{[-1]}|\geq\frac{1}{2}>0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

As the third, and final step, we use the Maxwell equation (
3.43
) to derive a useful identity relating
\upupsilon^{[{0}]}
with
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}
and
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Fix
M>0
and let
|a|\leq M
. For
\upupsilon^{[{0}]}
,
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
and
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}
as above, we have the pointwise identity

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\upupsilon^{[{0}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\frac{ia\kappa\sin\theta}{2\sqrt{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3/2}}\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{ia\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\left[\frac{\kappa}{2}e_{3}^{\rm as}\left(\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)+\frac{a\kappa}{2Mr_{+}}\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\frac{(r-r_{+})}{\kappa(r_{+})}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right]\,,

(6.21)

where we have introduced the notation

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{[-1]}:=\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i\Sigma(r_{+})}{2Mr_{+}\sin\theta}Z-\frac{ia\sin\theta}{\kappa(r_{+})}+\cot\theta\right)\,.

Remark 6.16
.

We note that, schematically, identity (
6.21
) has the form

e_{3}^{\rm as}\upupsilon^{[{0}]}=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{[-1]}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\dots

where the ellipses represent either terms in which
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}
and its
(e_{3}^{\rm as},e_{2}^{\rm as},e_{1}^{\rm as})
-derivatives are multiplied by functions of
(r,\theta)
which, if
|a|=M
, vanish at least linearly as
r\to r_{+}
, or terms proportional to
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.15
.

We recall identity (
6.17
) and the notation from the proof of Lemma
6.12
. By using the spin-weighted Maxwell equation (
3.43
), we obtain

\displaystyle\underline{L}\upupsilon^{[{0}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})}\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}Z+ia\sin\theta T+\cot\theta\right)\left(\frac{\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}}{\kappa}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i\Sigma}{(r^{2}+a^{2})\sin\theta}Z-\frac{ia\sin\theta}{\kappa}+\cot\theta\right)\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+\frac{ia\sin\theta\kappa}{2\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\left(\underline{L}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}+w\Phi^{[{-1}]}\right).

To conclude, we simply compare the operator acting on the first term on the right hand side with
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

6.4.3
Combining the key lemmas

We now combine the three key lemmas of Section
6.4.2
to estimate the quantity (
6.12
), thus extending Proposition
6.11
to the full
|a|\leq M
range.

Proposition 6.17
.

Fix
M>0
and let
0<\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{4}M
. Then, there exists a uniform constant
C=C(M,\epsilon)>0
such that, for any
|a|\leq M
, the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}e_{3}^{\rm as}(\upupsilon^{[{0}]})\,drd\Omega-{\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\frac{\kappa(r_{+})}{\sqrt{4Mr_{+}}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{[-1]}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\Phi^{[{-1}]}}\,d\Omega\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{X}\in\{\mathbb{X}_{1},\mathbb{X}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{[-1]}\}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}|\mathfrak{X}\Phi^{[{-1}]}|^{2}drd\Omega+\sum_{\mathfrak{X}\in\{\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{0}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{[-1]}\}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}|\mathfrak{X}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}|^{2}drd\Omega\right)

(6.22)

holds, where
\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}
and
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{[-1]}
are defined above in Lemmas
6.14
and
6.15
respectively and

\mathbb{X}_{0}
,
\mathbb{X}_{1}
denote the collections of vector fields

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta e_{3}^{\rm as},e_{4}^{\rm as},Z,1\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta^{2}\left(e_{3}^{\rm as}\right)^{2},\Delta e_{3}^{\rm as},(r-r_{+})^{2}Z^{2},(r-r_{+})Z,(r-M)\}\,.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we assume
r\leq 9M/4
. In view of Lemma
6.14
, we let
\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\colon L^{2}(d\Omega)\to L^{2}(d\Omega)
be the inverse of the differential operator
\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}
. From Lemma
6.12
, we readily derive the identity

\displaystyle\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle=-e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\left[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\left(\frac{1}{4}L-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}-\frac{w^{\prime}}{4w}\right)+a(Z+i\cos\theta)\right]\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{4}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta e_{3}^{\rm as}\underline{L}+\frac{a\Sigma}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Ze_{3}^{\rm as}+ia\cos\theta e_{3}^{\rm as}\right)\left(\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+aw\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\left[a-\frac{a^{2}r\sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})}{M}Z\right]\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{a^{2}}{Mr_{+}}\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\left[\left(Z+i\cos\theta\right)-\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{2Mr_{+}}\left(1-\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}Z\right)\right]Z\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\,.

(6.23)

By combining the identities (
6.21
) and (
6.23
), we obtain

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\upupsilon^{[{0}]}

\displaystyle=-e_{3}^{\rm as}\left(\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\Phi^{[{-1}]}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{a\kappa}{\sqrt{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3/2}}\left\{\frac{i\sin\theta}{2}\Phi^{[{-1}]}+\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\left[a\sin^{2}\theta\left(\frac{1}{4}L-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}-\frac{w^{\prime}}{4w}\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\left.\left.+(Z+i\cos\theta)-\frac{(a+ir\cos\theta)}{\kappa}\right]\right\}\Phi^{[{-1}]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{a\kappa}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\left\{\frac{i\sin\theta}{2}e_{3}^{\rm as}+\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{4}a\sin^{2}\theta e_{3}^{\rm as}\underline{L}+\frac{\Sigma}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Ze_{3}^{\rm as}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\left.\left.\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}+i\cos\theta e_{3}^{\rm as}\right)\right\}\left(\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{a\kappa(r-r_{+})}{Mr_{+}\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\left\{i\sin\theta\left[\frac{a(r+r_{+})}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}Z+\frac{Mr_{+}}{\kappa(r_{+})\kappa}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{a(r+r_{+})}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\left[\left(Z+i\cos\theta\right)-\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{2Mr_{+}}\left(1-\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}Z\right)\right]Z\right\}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{a\kappa w}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\left[a-\frac{a^{2}r\sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Z+\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})}{M}Z\right]\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\,,

(6.24)

where we used the commutation relations
[e_{3}^{\rm as},\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}]=0=[e_{3}^{\rm as},\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}]
holding for
r\leq 9M/4
(see Section
2.1.1
). By integrating identity (
6.24
) over
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}
-spheres and over the interval
r\in[r_{+},r_{+}+\epsilon]
, we deduce that, for
\epsilon\leq M/4
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r}}e_{3}^{\rm as}(\upupsilon^{[{0}]})drd\Omega-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\frac{\kappa(r_{+})}{\sqrt{4Mr_{+}}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{[-1]}\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}\Phi^{[{-1}]}d\Omega\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim_{\epsilon}\frac{|a|}{M}\sum_{\mathfrak{X}\in\{\mathring{\not{\nabla}},\mathrm{id}\}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}\left[\left(\Delta^{2}|e_{3}^{\rm as}\mathfrak{X}\Phi^{[{-1}]}|^{2}+|e_{4}^{\rm as}\mathfrak{X}\Phi^{[{-1}]}|^{2}+|Z\mathfrak{X}\Phi^{[{-1}]}|^{2}\right)\right]drd\Omega

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\frac{|a|}{M}\sum_{\mathfrak{X}\in\{\mathring{\not{\nabla}},\mathrm{id}\}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}\left[\Delta^{4}|(e_{3}^{\rm as})^{2}\mathfrak{X}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}|^{2}+\Delta^{2}|e_{3}^{\rm as}\mathfrak{X}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}|^{2}\right]drd\Omega

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\frac{|a|}{M}\sum_{\mathfrak{X}\in\{\mathring{\not{\nabla}},\mathrm{id}\}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}\left[(r-r_{+})^{4}|Z^{2}\mathfrak{X}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}|^{2}+(r-r_{+})^{2}|Z\mathfrak{X}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}|^{2}\right]drd\Omega

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\sum_{\mathfrak{X}\in\{\mathring{\not{\nabla}},\mathrm{id}\}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}\left[\left(\frac{|a|}{M}+(r-M)^{2}\right)|\mathfrak{X}\Phi^{[{-1}]}|^{2}+\frac{|a|}{M}(r-M)^{2}|\mathfrak{X}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}|^{2}\right]drd\Omega,

as claimed. In the estimates, the implicit constants depend on the norm of the inverse operator
\big(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1}
obtained in Lemma
6.14
.
∎

We now return to our original tensorial notation to control the first two terms on the right hand side of the estimates in Proposition
6.9
in terms of degenerate-at-
\mathcal{H}^{+}
energy norms of the extremal Maxwell components
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
and conclude the proof of Theorem
6.2
.

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

We combine Propositions
6.9
and
6.10
with Proposition
6.17
. By appealing to the isomorphism of Lemma
3.2
, one can express Proposition
6.17
in our original tensorial notation.
∎

6.5
Horizon conservation laws in the
|a|=M
case

In the extremal
|a|=M
case, Lemmas
6.12
and
6.15
allow one to derive conservation laws for the middle Maxwell components along the event horizon (i.e. at
r=r_{+}=|a|=M
).

We consider an axisymmetric Teukolsky field
\upalpha^{[{-1}]}
, i.e. such that
Z\upalpha^{[{-1}]}=0
, and introduce the notation

\displaystyle\upupsilon^{[{0}]}_{0}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\upupsilon^{[{0}]}d\Omega\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0,L}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}S_{0,L}^{[-1]}d\Omega\,,\qquad\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\tau,r_{+}}}\Phi^{[{-1}]}S_{0,L}^{[-1]}d\Omega\,,\qquad L\geq 1\,,

where
S_{m,L}^{[s]}(\theta,\tilde{\phi}{}^{*})
denote the
s
-spin-weighted spherical harmonics already introduced in Proposition
6.11
. From identity (
6.21
), we have

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\big(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}\big)=\frac{1}{2M}\left(\partial_{\theta}-\frac{2ia\sin\theta}{M-ia\cos\theta}+\cot\theta\right)\left((M-ia\cos\theta)\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right)+\frac{(M\cos\theta-ia)\sin\theta}{8M^{2}}\Phi^{[{-1}]}

along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, which yields

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\rm as}\left(\upupsilon^{[{0}]}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{30}M}\left(\frac{ia}{M}\sqrt{5}\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{1}-\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{2}\right)+\frac{ia}{M}\frac{(\sqrt{16}+1)}{2\sqrt{6}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0,1}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{L\in 2\mathbb{\mathbb{N}}}\sqrt{\frac{(2L+1)}{4L(L+1)}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0,L}+\frac{ia}{M}\sum_{L\in 2\mathbb{\mathbb{N}}+1}\sqrt{\frac{(2L+1)}{4L(L+1)}}\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0,L}\,.

(6.25)

On the other hand, from identity (
6.14
), we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathring{\not{\triangle}}^{[-1]}+1\right)\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0}=-e_{3}^{\rm as}\left(\Phi^{[{-1}]}\right)+\frac{1}{16}\sin^{2}\theta(1+\cos^{2}\theta)e_{4}^{\rm as}\left(\Phi^{[{-1}]}\right)+\frac{ia\cos\theta}{2M^{2}}\Phi^{[{-1}]}

along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, from which we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0,L}

\displaystyle=-e_{3}^{\rm as}\left(\frac{1}{L(L+1)}\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L}\right)+\frac{1}{40L(L+1)}e_{4}^{\rm as}\left(\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L}\right)

(6.26)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L^{\prime}\geq 1}\frac{\sqrt{\left(2L+1\right)\left(2L^{\prime}+1\right)}}{L(L+1)}\left\{\frac{\sqrt{9}}{105}\begin{pmatrix}L&L^{\prime}&4\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}L&L^{\prime}&4\\
-1&+1&0\end{pmatrix}e_{4}^{\rm as}\left(\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L^{\prime}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{1}{14}\begin{pmatrix}L&L^{\prime}&2\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}L&L^{\prime}&2\\
-1&+1&0\end{pmatrix}e_{4}^{\rm as}\left(\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L^{\prime}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{ia}{M^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}L&L^{\prime}&1\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}L&L^{\prime}&1\\
-1&+1&0\end{pmatrix}\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L^{\prime}}\right\}

for any
L\in\mathbb{N}
, where we used the standard notation

\begin{pmatrix}j_{1}&j_{2}&j_{3}\\
-s_{1}&-s_{2}&-s_{3}\end{pmatrix}

to denote the so-called
3j
-symbol. Inserting (
6.26
) into (
6.25
), we deduce that the evolution of the spherical mean of
\upupsilon^{[{0}]}
along the event horizon is completely determined by
\Phi^{[{-1}]}
in an explicitly computable fashion.

These formulas allow one to prove conservation laws (and thus, non-decay) for transversal derivatives of
\upupsilon^{[{0}]}_{0}
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
provided transversal derivatives of
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0,L}
and
\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{L}
themselves satisfy suitable conservation laws. Indeed, work
[
26
]
obtains conservation laws for
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0,1}
and
\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{1}
; it is expected, see e.g.
[
3
]
, that conservation laws for
\tilde{\upphi}^{[{-1}]}_{0,1}
and
\Phi^{[{-1}]}_{1}
with
L\geq 2
can also be derived.

7
Analysis of the extremal Maxwell components

In this section, we state energy boundedness and decay for the extremal Maxwell components.

7.1
The
|a|<M
case after
[
35
]

Works
[
34
,
35
]
obtain energy boundedness and decay for general solutions to the Teukolsky equations (
3.27
). Using Lemma
3.2
, one can translate those statements into energy boundedness and decay for the extremal Maxwell components.

Theorem 7.1

(
[
34
,
35
]
)
.

Fix
M>0
and
a_{0}\in[0,M)
. Let
J\geq 1
,
p\in[0,2)
and
\eta\in(0,1)
. Then, there exists a uniform constant
C=C(a_{0},M,J,p,\eta)>0
such that, for any
|a|\leq a_{0}
and any solution

\bm{\mathfrak{S}}=(\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}],\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}],\widehat{\bm{\rho}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}],\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}])

to the modified Maxwell equations (
3.32
)–(
3.37
) arising from seed initial data prescribed on
\Sigma_{0}
, the following energy estimates hold.

•

Non-degenerate energy boundedness: we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{J}\big[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)

\displaystyle\leq C\,\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{J}\big[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](0)\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{J}\big[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)

\displaystyle\leq C\,\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{J}_{p}\big[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](0)

for all
\tau\geq 0
.

•

Non-degenerate energy decay: we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{J}\big[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+\tau)^{2-\eta}}\,\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{2-\eta}^{J+2}\big[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](0)\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{J}\big[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+\tau)^{2-\eta}}\,\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{J+2}_{2-\eta}\big[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](0)

for all
\tau\geq 0
.

•

Energy decay at the event horizon: we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{1}\big[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau,\infty)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+\tau)^{2-\eta}}\,\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{2-\eta}^{3}\big[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](0)\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{1}\big[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](\tau,\infty)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+\tau)^{2-\eta}}\,\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{2-\eta}^{3}\big[r\bm{\underline{\alpha}}[\bm{\mathfrak{S}}]\big](0)

for all
\tau\geq 0
.

Remark 7.2
.

The proof of Theorem
7.1
in
[
34
,
35
]
makes use of the spin-weighted version of the Teukolsky equation (
3.27
), rather than the tensorial version of Proposition
3.1
, in the analysis. We direct the reader to
[
35
, Theorem 5.1]
for (energy) boundedness estimates and
[
35
, Corollary 5.2 and Theorem 9.3]
for (energy) decay estimates in the spin-weighted notation, and to Section
4.1.1
for a quick summary of how to translate between the tensorial and spin-weighted notations.

7.2
The
|a|\approx M
case: a conjectural statement for bounded azimuthal number

In Theorem
7.1
, we have
C(a_{0})\to\infty
as
a_{0}\to M
, and thus the energy boundedness and decay statements for the extremal Maxwell components break down towards extremality. As already noted in Section
5
, it is nonetheless expected that boundedness and (weaker) decay for the extremal Maxwell components still hold in the nearly extremal
|a|\approx M
case. The formulation of this expectation is the content of Conjecture
5.6
. In this section, we shall review recent literature concerning scalar fields on Kerr spacetimes in the (nearly) extremal case and comment on the results motivating the formulation of Conjecture
5.6
.

A simpler toy problem for understanding the Teukolsky equation (
3.27
) is the scalar wave equation. The first results on the behavior of scalar waves on extremal black holes are due to Aretakis. They showed that there are conservation laws for certain transverse derivatives of axisymmetric scalar field along
\mathcal{H}^{+}

[
7
]
but that, though these conservation laws may lead to a slower decay rate for the axisymmetric scalar field itself, it does decay
[
6
]
. Indeed, for initial data compactly supported away from
r=r_{+}
, axisymmetric scalar waves are expected to decay at a rate
(1+\tau)^{-2}
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and faster (on constant
r
hypersurfaces) away from it
[
3
,
12
]
.

Outside of axisymmetry, Casals, Gralla and Zimmerman
[
12
]
used a heuristic argument (based on the assumption that the worse behavior is related to the so-called superradiant threshold, cf. Remark
5.8
above) to compute the expected sharp decay rates for scalar fields supported on a fixed, but not necessarily zero, azimuthal mode
m
. They found that, even with compactly initial data supported away from
r=r_{+}
, the leading order trace of the scalar field on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
at late-times had a significantly slower decay rate,
(1+\tau)^{-1/2}
, if
m\neq 0
. Very recently, these heuristics were made rigorous in a remarkable work of Gajic
[
19
]
, which not only confirms that
(1+\tau)^{-1/2}
is the fastest decay one can hope for along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
for general scalar fields on extremal Kerr but moreover obtains
13
13

13

Strictly speaking, this is conditional on global, qualitative integrability assumptions for the scalar field, see
[
19
, Remark 1.1]
.
a precise, explicit, pointwise form for the late-time leading order contribution to the scalar field along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

In two follow-up works
[
25
,
13
]
, Casals, Gralla and Zimmerman extended their analysis to the Teukolsky equation (
3.27
). They found that, along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, one has
\bm{\alpha}\sim(1+\tau)^{-3/2}
but
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\sim(1+\tau)^{1/2}
, i.e. growth for
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
! Their work again suggests an explicit form for the late-time, near-horizon,
\upalpha^{\pm 1}
: for instance, using Lemma
3.2
to convert to tensorial notation, they propose

\displaystyle(r-M)\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\sim(1+\tau)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,\quad(r-M)\not{\nabla}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\sim(1+\tau)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,\quad(r-M)^{3/2}\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\sim 1\,,\quad\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\omega}\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\sim(1+\tau)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

for initial data supported on a fixed azimuthal mode and compactly supported away from horizon. Note that these rates are consistent with the boundedness and decay estimates in Conjecture
5.6
. In view of the previous paragraph, it seems likely that these claims, and thus Conjecture
5.6
, can be made rigorous by following the techniques introduced by
[
19
]
.

Remark 7.3
.

As discussed above, most of the literature on extremal Kerr,
|a|=M
, or nearly extremal Kerr
|a|\approx M
, deals with the case of solutions to the fixed azimuthal mode,
m
, solutions to the Teukolsky and scalar wave equations. Understanding how to deal with the limit
m\to\infty
, and thus with general initial data, is a major open problem already for scalar wave equations. Therefore, we purposely exclude this regime from Conjecture
5.6
.

8
Proof of the main theorems

In this section, we combine the analysis of the middle Maxwell components of Section
6
with that of the extremal Maxwell components of Section
7
to prove the two main theorems of the paper.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

In view of Theorem
7.1
, both the qualitative assumption for
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
and the quantitative assumption for
\bm{\alpha}
in Theorem
6.1
hold true, where one identifies
\mathbb{D}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}](0)=\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{2-\eta}^{3}[r^{2}\bm{\alpha}](0)
and
p_{\alpha}=1-\eta
. Combining Theorem
7.1
with Theorem
6.1
yields the conclusion.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.9
.

In view of Conjecture
5.6
, both the qualitative assumption for
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
and the quantitative assumption for
\bm{\alpha}
in Theorem
6.2
hold true. Combining Conjecture
5.6
with Theorem
6.2
yields the conclusion.
∎

Appendix A
Further equations and identities from the Maxwell system

In this appendix, we derive several identities which are used in the paper. The identities are derived starting from the Maxwell equations.

For later convenience, we recall the commutation identities

\displaystyle[e_{3}^{\rm as},e_{A}^{\rm as}]

\displaystyle=\not{\underline{M}}{}_{A}^{B}e_{B}^{\rm as}-{\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}}{}^{B}_{A}e_{B}^{\rm as}

\displaystyle=\not{\underline{M}}{}_{A}^{B}e_{B}^{\rm as}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}e_{A}^{\rm as}-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){\not{\varepsilon}_{A}}^{B}e_{B}^{\rm as}\,,

(A.1)

\displaystyle[e_{4}^{\rm as},e_{A}^{\rm as}]

\displaystyle=\not{M}{}_{A}^{B}e_{B}^{\rm as}-{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}}{}^{B}_{A}e_{B}^{\rm as}+(\eta+\underline{\eta})_{A}e_{4}^{\rm as}

\displaystyle=\not{M}{}_{A}^{B}e_{B}^{\rm as}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi e_{A}^{\rm as}-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){\not{\varepsilon}_{A}}^{B}e_{B}^{\rm as}+(\eta+\underline{\eta})_{A}e_{4}^{\rm as}\,,

(A.2)

which are obtained by specialising the general commutation formulae of
[
10
, Section 4.6]
under the identification of
(\bm{\mathcal{M}},\bm{g})
with the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
and
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bm{\mathcal{N}}}
with the algebraically special horizontal distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
(see, in particular,
[
10
, Equations (74)–(75)]
). We also recall the commutation identities

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{3},\divsl]\varsigma

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\divsl\varsigma+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\curlsl\varsigma-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\eta\wedge\varsigma\,,

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{3},\curlsl]\varsigma

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\curlsl\varsigma-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\divsl\varsigma

for any
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-form
\varsigma
(see
[
10
, Equation (142)]
).

A.1
The tensorial Teukolsky–Starobinsky identities

In the following proposition, we show that the extremal Maxwell components
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
are related by differential identities akin to the Weyl identities for linearized gravity, see e.g.
[
36
]
, or the Teukolsky–Starobinsky identities
[
37
,
39
]
in the spin-weighted formalism.

Proposition A.1

(Teukolsky–Starobinsky)
.

For any solution to the Maxwell equations (
3.13
)–(
3.18
), the identities

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{\Sigma}\left(\not{\nabla}_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{3}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\,\,{}^{\star}\right)\left[\Sigma\left(\not{\nabla}_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\,\,{}^{\star}\right)\,\bm{\alpha}\right]\\
&\quad\qquad=-(\not{\nabla}+2\eta)\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\eta)\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\,,\end{split}

(A.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(\not{\nabla}_{4}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi+\frac{3}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\,\,{}^{\star}\right)\left[\Sigma\left(\not{\nabla}_{4}+\,\hat{\omega}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\,\,{}^{\star}\right)\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\right]\\
&\quad\qquad=-\left(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta}\right)\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-{}^{\star}\left(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta}\right)\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})\end{split}

(A.4)

hold.

Proof.

We start by taking a
\not{\nabla}_{3}
-derivative of equation (
3.23
). By using the commutation identity (
A.1
), we compute

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\left[\Sigma(\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\alpha}+\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\alpha})\right]

\displaystyle\quad=(\not{\nabla}+\,\eta-\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+\,\eta-\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})+[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}](\Sigma\bm{\rho})+[\not{\nabla}_{3},{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}](\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\not{\nabla}_{3}(\eta-\underline{\eta})(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+\not{\nabla}_{3}({}^{\star}\eta-{}^{\star}\underline{\eta})(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle\quad=(\not{\nabla}+\,\eta-\underline{\eta})\left[-\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta},\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\right]+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+\,\eta-\underline{\eta})\left[-\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-\left[{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})-\not{\nabla}_{3}(\eta-\underline{\eta})+{}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\right](\Sigma\bm{\rho})+\left[\not{\nabla}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})+\not{\nabla}_{3}({}^{\star}\eta-{}^{\star}\underline{\eta})+(\eta-\underline{\eta})(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\right](\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}](\Sigma\bm{\rho})+[\not{\nabla}_{3},{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}](\Sigma\bm{\sigma})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star}\left[\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})\right]

\displaystyle\quad=(\not{\nabla}+\,\eta-\underline{\eta})\left[-\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta},\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\right]+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+\,\eta-\underline{\eta})\left[-\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{3}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\Sigma{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\alpha}+\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\alpha})-\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\Sigma(\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\alpha}+\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\alpha})\,,

where we also applied the geometric identities of Section
2.2.4
. We thus obtain

\displaystyle\left(\not{\nabla}_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{3}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\,\,{}^{\star}\right)\left[\Sigma\left(\not{\nabla}_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\,\,{}^{\star}\right)\,\bm{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\qquad=(\not{\nabla}+\,\eta-\underline{\eta})\left[-\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta},\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\right]+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+\,\eta-\underline{\eta})\left[-\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\right].

To conclude the proof of (
A.3
), we note

\displaystyle-\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta},\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})=-\Sigma\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\,,\quad\quad-\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})=-\Sigma\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\,,

\displaystyle(\not{\nabla}+\eta-\underline{\eta})(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})=\Sigma(\not{\nabla}+\eta-\underline{\eta})\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\underline{\alpha}})=\Sigma(\not{\nabla}+2\eta)\bm{\underline{\alpha}}\,.

To derive the identity (
A.4
), we take a
\not{\nabla}_{4}
-derivative of equation (
3.24
). By using the commutation identity (
A.2
) and the geometric identities of Section
2.2.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\left[\Sigma(\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\omega}\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\right]

\displaystyle\quad=-(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})-\left([\not{\nabla}_{4},\not{\nabla}]-(\eta+\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)(\Sigma\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\left([\not{\nabla}_{4},{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}]-{}^{\star}(\eta+\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})+\not{\nabla}_{4}(\eta-\underline{\eta})(\Sigma\bm{\rho})-\not{\nabla}_{4}({}^{\star}\eta-{}^{\star}\underline{\eta})(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle\quad=-(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\left[\not{\nabla}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})-\not{\nabla}_{4}({}^{\star}\eta-{}^{\star}\underline{\eta})+2(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}\underline{\eta}\right](\Sigma\bm{\rho})+\left[{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})+\not{\nabla}_{4}(\eta-\underline{\eta})+2(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\underline{\eta}\right](\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-\left([\not{\nabla}_{4},\not{\nabla}]-(\eta+\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+\left([\not{\nabla}_{4},{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}]-{}^{\star}(\eta+\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\right)(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}\left[-\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})\right]

\displaystyle\quad=-(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{3}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\Sigma{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\omega}\bm{\underline{\alpha}})-\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\Sigma(\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\omega}\bm{\underline{\alpha}})\,,

from which the identity (
A.4
) follows.
∎

A.2
The tensorial Teukolsky equations

In Proposition
3.1
, we stated that, for any solution to the Maxwell equations, the extremal Maxwell components
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
each satisfy a decoupled wave equation akin to the spin-weighted Teukolsky equations (
3.27
)
[
40
]
. In this section, we prove Proposition
3.1
, i.e. we derive the tensorial Teukolsky equations (
3.25
)–(
3.26
).

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

By using the Maxwell equations (
3.13
)–(
3.18
) and the geometric identities of Section
2.2.4
, we compute

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}(\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\alpha}+\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle\quad=[\not{\nabla}_{4},\not{\nabla}]\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}[\not{\nabla}_{4},\not{\nabla}]\bm{\sigma}+(\not{\nabla}+2\eta)\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\eta)\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\sigma}+2\not{\nabla}_{4}\eta\bm{\rho}+2\not{\nabla}_{4}({}^{\star}\eta)\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad=(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\sigma}+2\not{\nabla}_{4}\eta\bm{\rho}+2\not{\nabla}_{4}({}^{\star}\eta)\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\Big\{[\not{\nabla}_{4},\not{\nabla}]-(\eta+\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\Big\}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}\Big\{[\not{\nabla}_{4},\not{\nabla}]-(\eta+\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{4}\Big\}\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad=(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})(\divsl\bm{\alpha}+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\cdot\bm{\alpha})-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})(\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\left[2\not{\nabla}_{4}\eta-(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})\tr\chi+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\right]\bm{\rho}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\left[2\not{\nabla}_{4}({}^{\star}\eta)-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})\tr\chi-(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi})\right]\bm{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\left[3\tr\chi-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}\right]\left(\not{\nabla}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}\bm{\sigma}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})(\divsl\bm{\alpha}+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\cdot\bm{\alpha})-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})(\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-\left[\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot{\chi}){}^{\star}\right]\left(\not{\nabla}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}\bm{\sigma}+2\eta\bm{\rho}+2{}^{\star}\eta\bm{\sigma}\right)

and

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}(\not{\nabla}_{4}\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\bm{\underline{\alpha}}+\hat{\omega}\bm{\underline{\alpha}})

\displaystyle\quad=-[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}]\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}]\bm{\sigma}-(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}_{3}\bm{\sigma}-2\not{\nabla}_{3}\underline{\eta}\bm{\rho}+2\not{\nabla}_{3}({}^{\star}\eta)\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad=(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{2}\left[3\tr\underline{\chi}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\right]\left(-\not{\nabla}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}\bm{\sigma}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\left[-2\not{\nabla}_{3}\underline{\eta}+(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\tr\underline{\chi}-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\right]\bm{\rho}+\left[2\not{\nabla}_{3}({}^{\star}\underline{\eta})-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\tr\underline{\chi}-(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\right]\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-\Big\{[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}]+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\underline{\chi}+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star})\not{\nabla}\Big\}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}\Big\{[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}]+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\underline{\chi}+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star})\not{\nabla}\Big\}\bm{\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad=(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\divsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-{}^{\star}(\not{\nabla}+2\underline{\eta})\curlsl\bm{\underline{\alpha}}-\left[\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star}\right]\left(-\not{\nabla}\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}\bm{\sigma}-2\underline{\eta}\bm{\rho}+2{}^{\star}\underline{\eta}\bm{\sigma}\right)\,.

To conclude the proof, we note

\displaystyle\divsl\bm{\alpha}+(\eta+\underline{\eta},\bm{\alpha})=\Sigma^{-1}\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})\,,\quad\quad\curlsl\bm{\alpha}+(\eta+\underline{\eta})\wedge\bm{\alpha}=\Sigma^{-1}\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})\,,

\displaystyle(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})(\Sigma^{-1}\bm{\alpha})=\Sigma^{-1}(\not{\nabla}+3\eta+\underline{\eta})\bm{\alpha}-\Sigma^{-2}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})=\Sigma^{-1}(\not{\nabla}+2\eta)\bm{\alpha}

and similarly for
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
.
∎

A.3
The tensorial Fackerell–Ipser equations

Similarly to the extremal Maxwell components
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\underline{\alpha}}
, the middle Maxwell components
\bm{\rho}
and
\bm{\sigma}
satisfy (now coupled) wave equations.

Proposition A.2
.

For any solution to the Maxwell equations (
3.13
)–(
3.18
), the identities

\displaystyle\left[\not{\nabla}_{3}\not{\nabla}_{4}-\not{\triangle}-(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot\not{\nabla}\right](\Sigma\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=-\underline{\chi}\cdot(\eta\otimes(\Sigma\bm{\alpha}))-\left[(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\not{\nabla}_{3}+{}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot\not{\nabla}-2\sigma_{G}\right](\Sigma\bm{\sigma})\,,

\displaystyle\left[\not{\nabla}_{3}\not{\nabla}_{4}-\not{\triangle}-(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot\not{\nabla}\right](\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\underline{\chi}\wedge(\eta\otimes(\Sigma\bm{\alpha}))+\left[(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\not{\nabla}_{3}-{}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot\not{\nabla}-2\sigma_{G}\right](\Sigma\bm{\rho})

hold. We recall the definition of
\sigma_{G}
from Section
2.2.4
.

Proof.

We take a
\not{\nabla}_{3}
-derivative of equation (
3.22
) and obtain

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\not{\nabla}_{4}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=[\not{\nabla}_{3},\divsl](\Sigma\bm{\alpha})+\divsl\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-\not{\nabla}_{3}\left((\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\Sigma\bm{\rho})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\divsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\curlsl(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\eta\wedge(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})+\frac{1}{2}\divsl\left(\tr\underline{\chi}\Sigma\bm{\alpha}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}){}^{\star}(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\divsl\left(\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})\right)+\divsl\left((\eta-\underline{\eta})\Sigma\bm{\rho}+{}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\Sigma\bm{\sigma}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\not{\nabla}_{3}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\Sigma\bm{\rho})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\eta\wedge(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\nabla}\tr\underline{\chi})\cdot(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})-\frac{1}{2}\not{\nabla}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\wedge(\Sigma\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle\qquad+\not{\triangle}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot[\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})-{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})]+(2\curlsl\eta-\not{\nabla}_{3}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}))(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=-\underline{\chi}\cdot(\eta\otimes(\Sigma\bm{\alpha}))+\not{\triangle}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})+(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot[\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})-{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})]+2\sigma_{G}\Sigma\bm{\sigma}-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})\,.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\not{\nabla}_{4}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\underline{\chi}\wedge(\eta\otimes(\Sigma\bm{\alpha}))+\not{\triangle}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})-{}^{\star}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot[\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})-{}^{\star}\not{\nabla}(\Sigma\bm{\sigma})]-2\sigma_{G}\Sigma\bm{\rho}+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\not{\nabla}_{3}(\Sigma\bm{\rho})\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

It was shown by Fackerell and Ipser
[
18
]
that, in the Newman–Penrose formalism, the middle Maxwell components satisfy a
decoupled
wave equation with a complex potential. By introducing
\upupsilon^{[{0}]}
as defined in (
3.39
), one obtains the following.

Proposition A.3

(Fackerell–Ipser equation)
.

For any solution to the Maxwell equations (
3.40
)–(
3.43
), the identity

\displaystyle\Box_{g}(\kappa^{-1}\upupsilon^{[{0}]})+\frac{2M}{(r-ia\cos\theta)^{3}}(\kappa^{-1}\upupsilon^{[{0}]})=0

(A.5)

holds.

A.4
The Maxwell equations for transformed spin-weighted quantities

In light of the definitions (
4.1.1
)–(
4.2
) (originally introduced in
[
34
,
35
]
), it is convenient to recast the Maxwell equations (
3.40
)–(
3.43
) as

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\overline{\kappa}m(\upupsilon^{[{0}]})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\left(\Phi^{[{+1}]}+\frac{a(a+ir\cos\theta)}{\kappa}\upphi^{[{+1}]}_{0}\right),

(A.6)

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\kappa\overline{m}(\upupsilon^{[{0}]})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}}\left(\Phi^{[{-1}]}-\frac{a(a+ir\cos\theta)}{\kappa}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right),

(A.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\,e_{4}^{\rm as}(\upupsilon^{[{0}]})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})}(\sqrt{2}\kappa\overline{m}+\cot\theta)\left(\frac{\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}}{\kappa}\upphi^{[{+1}]}_{0}\right),

(A.8)

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\,e_{3}^{\rm as}(\upupsilon^{[{0}]})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})}(\sqrt{2}\overline{\kappa}m+\cot\theta)\left(\frac{\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}}{\kappa}\upphi^{[{-1}]}_{0}\right),

(A.9)

where
m=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(e_{1}^{\rm as}+ie_{2}^{\rm as})
for the choice of
(e_{1}^{\rm as},e_{2}^{\rm as})
given in (
2.13
).
